## CHAPTER VI.

### On the Essence of *Carabé*, or Amber.

I have been in doubt as to which **kingdom** <note>Refers to the "Three Kingdoms of Nature": Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral</note> I should place this operation, although **Amber** <term>Carabé: An older term for yellow amber, derived from the Arabic word 'kahraba'</term> is well enough known, especially since authors speak of it in various ways. Some place it in the rank of **Gums** <note>implying a plant origin, like tree resin</note>, others among the number of **Minerals**, especially since it is found mixed with gold. For my part, I am of the opinion of those

->Charitable & easy. 167<-

...[those] who consider it a <term>Bitumen<note>A naturally occurring, oily, or tar-like mineral substance. The author is debating whether Amber is a resin or a mineral.</note></term>, because of the strong odor it gives off when it is burned. This is what compels me to place it in the rank of Minerals. Take white or yellow Amber, and place it in a <term>Retort<note>original: "Cornuë"; a glass bulb with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</note></term>, filled at most halfway: distill with a <term>Sand bath<note>original: "feu de sable"; a method of heating a vessel evenly by nesting it in a container of sand.</note></term>; and when smoke no longer emerges from the Retort, cease your operation. If some water is found with the essence, you will separate it using a separatory vessel.

This oil, or essence, is very useful. It cures apoplexy <note>A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</note>, three drops taken in a spoonful of wine, or sweetened water. It provokes urine, and causes the expulsion of <term>sand<note>Small kidney or bladder stones.</note></term> in parsley or fennel water. It is good for the <term>Falling sickness<note>original: "mal caduc"; an old name for epilepsy.</note></term>, and for <term>Suffocations of the womb<note>original: "suffocations de matrice"; a historical diagnosis often related to hysteria or uterine distress.</note></term>, and convulsions, [taken] in sage water: applied externally, it heals paralytics, wounds and ulcers, and brings out bone splinters. It comforts the stomach, and stops <term>diarrhea<note>original: "dévoyement"; a loosening of the bowels.</note></term>, if one rubs the said parts with it.

#### CHAPTER VII.

*On Coral.*

*On the Tincture of Coral.*

**T**AKE the reddest Coral, and reduce it into small pieces, and put them into a Matrass <term>a long-necked glass flask used for digestion and distillation</term>: pour over it filtered lemon juice, so that it floats four fingers' breadth above <note>a traditional measurement of depth, roughly three to four inches</note>: seal your vessel, and set it to digest in a Water Bath <term>also known as a Bain-marie, used for gentle heating</term>, and leave it there until you see your liquid turn red. Then you shall separate it by decanting <term>the process of pouring off liquid while leaving the sediment at the bottom</term>, and add more lemon juice over your remaining Coral, and set it to digest as you did before, and continue until your juice no longer draws out any tincture. Take all the tinctures; for every pound of these you shall add two pounds of sugar, and boil it until it reaches the consistency of a syrup.
      This tincture is excellent for

### CHAPTER VIII.

### Concerning Antimony.

### Concerning Crocus of Antimony.

The **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophes." In 17th-century medical texts, this usually refers to alchemists or "spagyric" physicians who followed the traditions of Paracelsus.</note> have processed **Antimony** in so many ways that one could write several volumes about their operations: I will restrict myself to three or four, whose effects are certain and proven through experience. Take equal parts of **Antimony** and **Saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, used in this process to cause a rapid chemical reaction (deflagration) with the antimony.</term>; powder each one separately, then mix them together, and put a spoonful of the mixture into a cast-iron mortar over glowing coals. Set fire to it with a coal, then cover the mortar with a fire shovel: And when the bubbling <note>The original French "ebulition" describes the violent chemical reaction or "flashing" that occurs when these two substances are ignited.</note> has subsided, stir with an iron rod, and put— <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

## Diaphoretic Antimony
<note>Diaphoretic Antimony (antimonium diaphoreticum) was a popular 17th-century chemical medicine. "Diaphoretic" means it was intended to induce sweating to "expel" illness from the body.</note>

**TAKE** <term>Antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-white metalloid that was a cornerstone of alchemical medicine, often used to induce purging or sweating.</note> & <term>Saltpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent to react with the antimony.</note> in equal parts; reduce them to powder, and put them in a **Crucible**, which you will cover with another Crucible pierced through the bottom. <term>Lute</term> <note>To "lute" is to seal the joints of the vessels with a paste made of clay, dung, or sand to make them airtight and heat-resistant.</note> them together, and when the lute is dry, place them in the midst of burning coals; a reaction will occur that makes a noise, just as in the previous operation. At the end of three hours, remove your Crucibles and take your material; reduce it to powder and mix it with as much Saltpeter as you put in previously. Put the whole mixture into luted Crucibles and place it in a burning fire for eighteen or twenty hours, and until the material is very white. Then you shall grind, wash, and filter it three or four times to remove the <term>acrimony</term> <note>The harsh, caustic, or bitter quality left behind by the saltpeter.</note> of the Saltpeter. The final water in which you wash it must be some <term>cordial water</term> <note>A medicinal liquid believed to be heart-strengthening or invigorating.</note>, such as Rose, Cinnamon, Anise, or Fennel.

This powder is **sudorific** <note>A substance that causes sweating.</note>; it purges through the urine and through sweat.

->H iiij<-
<note>This mark at the bottom of the page is a "signature," used by early modern printers to ensure the pages were folded and bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page provides a chemical recipe for Diaphoretic Antimony, a remedy produced by heating antimony and saltpeter in sealed crucibles over many hours. The resulting white powder was used in early modern medicine to induce sweating and urination to treat various ailments.</summary>

The dose is ten to fifteen <term>grains<note>A historical unit of weight based on the mass of a single seed of cereal; approximately 64 milligrams.</note></term> in rose conserve, or the pulp of cooked apples.

*Oil, or Syrup of Antimony.*

TAKE pulverized antimony as much as you wish; put it in a <term>crucible<note>A ceramic or metal container used for melting or calcining substances at very high temperatures.</note></term> over the fire, and leave it there for five or six hours, stirring it constantly with an iron rod, until the antimony has acquired a grayish color. Remove the crucible and let it cool: take the mass which you will reduce to powder, and mix it with an equal part of fine sugar. Put it all into a <term>retort<note>A laboratory vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for the distillation of substances.</note></term>, and distill it using a <term>fire of wheel<note>Original: "feu de roüe." An alchemical heating technique where fuel is arranged in a circle around the vessel to increase heat intensity.</note></term> until nothing more rises. This oil purges gently through the bowels and without violence: twelve to twenty drops placed in an infusion of one <term>drachm<note>Original: "gros." A unit of apothecary weight equal to roughly 3.8 grams.</note></term> of <term>senna<note>A plant-based laxative commonly used in early modern medicine.</note></term> cures <term>quartan and tertian fevers<note>Types of malaria-like fevers where the fever spikes recur every third or fourth day.</note></term>. It must be taken three times when the fevers are stubborn, leaving an interval of one or two days, according to the strength of the patient and the effect produced by the preceding remedy.

## Tincture of Antimony.

CRUSH some Antimony into an impalpable powder, and place it in a large earthenware vessel, which is not glazed, and which can withstand the fire: place it on a furnace or a brazier, and make a moderate fire. Stir the powder constantly with a small iron rod, until the sulfur of the Antimony is entirely consumed: which you will recognize when it no longer gives off smoke and a blue flame. This operation requires at least forty-eight hours <note>original: "deux fois vingt-quatre heures" or two times twenty-four hours</note>, and you must be careful of the smoke, because it is very harmful <note>The "smoke" mentioned here contains toxic antimony trioxide and sulfur dioxide</note>. When the powder has been dried in this manner, put it into a Matrass <term>Matrass: a glass flask with a round body and a long neck, used in distilling and digestion</term>, and pour spirit of wine <note>highly rectified alcohol or brandy</note> over it, so that it floats four fingers' width above; seal the vessel well, and put it to digest in a Bain-Marie <term>Bain-Marie: a water bath used to heat substances gently and evenly</term>, until it becomes red. Pour off the tinted spirit by inclination <note>decanting or tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid without disturbing the sediment</note>, and add more [spirit], and let it digest as before, and continue until the spirit no longer...

# CHAPTER IX.

## On Iron, or Mars.

### On the Crocus of Mars.

There is an infinite number of ways to extract the salt, or saffron of <term>Mars</term> <note>original: "Mars." In the language of early chemistry and alchemy, Mars is the name and symbol used for the metal iron.</note>, most of which are long and laborious. Here is a very easy one. Take some very clean steel filings and place them in a large earthenware dish: if you wish to make it <term>astringent</term> <note>a substance that causes the contraction of body tissues, often used to stop bleeding or secretions</note>, pour distilled vinegar over it; if it is to be an <term>aperitive</term> <note>original: "l’aperitif." In historical medicine, this refers to a remedy used to "open" the body's channels, such as the pores or the liver, to treat obstructions</note>, you will only use common water. Expose your vessel to the sun, and stir it five or six times a day. And when your liquid becomes red, pour it off by <term>inclination</term> <note>the process of tilting a vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the heavier sediment at the bottom</note> into another vessel while it is still cloudy, and add more vinegar or water over the filings, and return it to the sun as before, and continue until you have as much <term>tincture</term> <note>a medicinal extract dissolved in a liquid</note> as you desire. Take all your tinctures,

H vj <note>This "H vj" at the bottom of the page is a "printer’s signature," a mark used by bookbinders to ensure the pages were folded and gathered in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page provides a simplified pharmaceutical recipe for creating "Crocus of Mars" (iron oxide) by oxidizing steel filings in the sun using either vinegar or water.</summary>

Pour off what is on top by tilting the vessel. If it is the <term>astringent</term> <note>A substance that causes body tissues to contract, used to stop bleeding or secretions.</note> variety that you wish to make, calcine it in a <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>A furnace that reflects heat back onto the substance being heated.</note> between two crucibles for five or six hours. For the <term>aperitive</term> <note>In early modern medicine, a substance used to "open" the body's channels or remove obstructions.</note> variety, it is enough to dry it gently.

   The virtues of the astringent Crocus are to tighten and to dry; this is why it is used for dysentery, <term>lientery</term> <note>A type of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested.</note>, and other similar illnesses. The virtues of the aperitive are to thin the humors and to open obstructions. It is taken for <term>pale colors</term> <note>Historically known as "greensickness" or chlorosis, a form of anemia often affecting young women.</note> and to provoke <term>purgations</term> <note>In this medical context, likely referring to the menstrual cycle.</note>.

   *Another aperitive Crocus of Mars.*

**T**AKE equal parts of steel filings and crushed sulfur; put them into a crucible over burning coals, and stir constantly with an iron spatula until all the sulfur is burned and no longer produces a flame. Then add new sulfur, and stir as you <note>The text continues on the next page.</note>

# FIFTH PART

## FOREWORD

**I** do not claim at all to take credit for the remedies that I am bringing to light as being of my own composition: I admit that the greatest part consists of the prescriptions <term>prescriptions: originally 'ordonnances,' these were formal medical instructions for the preparation and use of drugs</term> of very distinguished Doctors of the Faculty of Paris <note>The Faculty of Medicine of Paris was the most influential medical body in France, responsible for licensing physicians and overseeing medical practice.</note>, who charitably prescribed them to poor patients, and which I have put into practice; these have succeeded most fortunately. Another portion was given to me by my friends. I also cannot deny that there are some of my own composition, the effectiveness of which is very certain. I can offer this assurance, as I have tested <note>The author uses 'experimentez,' implying they have personally witnessed the success of these treatments through trial and observation.</note> them all. In the preceding Treatises I have taught the man- <note>The text likely continues on the next page as 'the manner' or 'the way.'</note>

<summary>This page serves as an introduction to a collection of medical remedies, where the author acknowledges that most formulas were provided by elite Parisian physicians for the care of the poor.</summary>
<keywords>Medical prescriptions, Faculty of Paris, charitable medicine, clinical experience, 17th-century pharmacy</keywords>

...manner of operating, and in what way it was necessary to prepare the remedies; and I have given the virtues and the properties of several <term>Compounds</term> <note>The original term "Mixtes" refers to chemical combinations or medicinal mixtures made from various elements</note>. It remains for us now to put them into practice. This is what I intend to teach in this Part. I pray all persons who shall be relieved by these little remedies, to remember me in the prayers that they shall offer to God. This is the only favor that I ask of them.

### Water for blurry and clouded eyes.

**TAKE** Plantain, Rue, Fennel, Celandine, Marjoram, in equal parts, which you shall crush and extract the juices from. To two pounds of these juices, add one pound of white honey and one ounce of crude antimony <term>antimony: a metallic mineral (stibnite) used in early modern medicine for its supposed cleansing properties, though now known to be toxic</term> reduced to powder. Then distill in a water bath <term>Bain: a "bain-marie" or indirect water heater used to prevent scorching</term> over a low fire, for fear that the honey might swell, until nothing more rises. One must wash the eyes with this water evening and morning, and place a compress soaked in it over the eyes during the night.

### Water against ear pain.

### Water against melancholy.

## Water that produces milk for wet-nurses.

This syrup strengthens, purges, and purifies the blood, and refreshes the body: it preserves youth, and there are almost no diseases for which it does not serve as a remedy. ### Syrup to induce sleep.

**TAKE** juice of purslane and lettuce, two pounds of each; water lily flowers and <term>wild poppy</term> flowers (otherwise known as <term>Rhoeas</term><note>original: "Rheas," the Greek-derived botanical name for the red corn poppy, *Papaver rhoeas*.</note> or corn poppies), half a pound of each. Boil the juices and the flowers together for about a quarter of an hour; then filter them through a <term>straining bag</term><note>original: "chausse," a conical cloth filter, often called a "Hippocras bag," used by apothecaries to clarify liquids.</note> and add sugar, pound for pound <note>Meaning equal weights of liquid and sugar.</note>. Cook this until it reaches the consistency of a syrup. One must skim the foam off all syrups toward the end of their cooking. A one-ounce dose should be taken in the evening when going to bed, in a glass of barley water. It refreshes, induces sleep, and is excellent against inflammations of the lungs.

### Syrup that stops diarrhea, dysentery, and the bloody flux.

One must take lemon juice and pomegranate juice, one pound of each; two pounds of quince fruit: let the whole thing infuse for twelve hours with one <term>drachm<note>A historical apothecary weight, roughly equivalent to 3.9 grams or about 3/4 of a teaspoon of powder.</note></term> of cinnamon; then strain and add three <term>quarterons<note>A "quarteron" usually refers to a quarter-pound (4 ounces).</note></term> of sugar for every pound of juice. One should take this syrup two or three times a day, one ounce at a time in a glass of <term>iron water<note>Also known as chalybeate water; water that has been treated with iron, often by quenching hot iron in it, used for its astringent and fortifying properties.</note></term>. This must be continued for three or four consecutive days.

->**Refreshing Ptisan**<-

### Pills for asthma and chronic cough.

half a pound of pork fat, the yolks of thirty hard-boiled eggs, two drachms <term>drachm: an apothecary measurement equal to approximately 3.9 grams</term> of spirit of salt <term>spirit of salt: an early chemical name for hydrochloric acid</term>, two ounces of new wax: melt everything in a new glazed pot, and boil it for a quarter of an hour over a low flame, then strain it. It is also excellent against ulcers.

->Balm for the pains of the cold gout<-
<note>In the medical theories of the time, "cold gout" was believed to be caused by an excess of "cold" humors like phlegm, necessitating warming treatments.</note>

TAKE roots and leaves of Hyssop and Parsley, two pounds of each; remove the woody cores <note>original: cordes</note> from the roots, cut them into pieces, and place them in a glazed earthen pot with one pound of crushed Juniper berries, two pounds of white wine, and one pound of pork fat. Boil the entire mixture until the wine has evaporated <note>original: consommé</note>, then press out the liquid. This balm is applied as directed above. It is effective against all pains that result from the cold.

        *Minium Plaster.*
TAKE olive oil, one pound and a half; Minium <note>original: "Minium"; red lead or lead tetroxide, historically used in ointments for its drying and healing properties, despite its toxicity</note>, one pound: incorporate the Minium and the oil as you have done above, and work in the same manner, until it becomes a brownish red, then make it into rolls <term>magdaleons: small, cylindrical rolls of medical plaster that could be easily stored and sliced for application</term>. ...all in powder, a half-pound of each: heat the oil in a glazed earthenware pot; when it begins to boil, add the turpentine <note>original: "Therebentine"; a resinous liquid used historically for its antiseptic and adhesive properties</note>, and stir with a spatula until it is well melted; then incorporate the powders little by little, and let it boil until the whole reaches the consistency of a plaster <term>emplastre: a thick, sticky medicinal paste applied to the skin, usually spread on a cloth</term>. You must stir constantly until it is cold, and then make it into rolls. To remove dead flesh <note>necrotic tissue</note>, if the wound is deep, you must soak a lint pad <term>plumaceau: a small bundle or wad of lint, soft cloth, or sponge used to pack a wound or apply medicine</term> in the melted plaster, place it into the wound, and put another plaster over the top. To stop gangrene, you must scarify <note>make small, shallow incisions in the skin</note> or shred the wound down to the raw, living flesh, and apply the lint pads as previously described, along with a large plaster. You must continue this until all the dead flesh is removed and the living flesh returns.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;L vi <note>This is a "signature mark" used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of the book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page provides instructions for finishing a medicinal plaster and details how to use it to treat deep wounds, remove necrotic tissue, and arrest the spread of gangrene through scarification and packing.</summary>

Plaster against cuts, chapping, and fissures.

TAKE <term>rose oil</term> two pounds, <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "Ceruse"; this is lead carbonate. # CHAPTER ONE.

## Concerning simple distilled waters for the beautification of the face.

The <term>Simples</term> <note>medicinal herbs or plants used in their natural state</note> from which waters for the face are drawn are Plantain, Silverweed, Water Lily, Great Nettle, and Bryony <note>original: "Couleuvrée"</note>; the leaves of which must be crushed, fermented, pressed, and distilled in a <term>Bain-Marie</term> <note>a water bath used to heat substances gently and evenly</note>. These waters must be exposed to the sun to preserve them.

For flowers, they must be placed freshly picked into a <term>Cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still</note>, then distilled in a <term>Bain-Marie</term>, without adding any <term>menstruum</term> <note>original: "menstruë"; a solvent used to extract or dissolve substances</note>: it is enough to press them slightly. Usually, flowers of Rosemary, Almond, Peach, Bean, Elderberry, Lily of the Valley, Linden, Lily, and Marshmallow are used.

The waters distilled from fruits for the face are green Walnuts, Strawberries, Lemons, Melons, and Cucumbers,

## Water for coarse complexions.

**TAKE** a dozen Lemons that have thin rinds, the whites of a dozen fresh hard-boiled eggs, from which the yolks have been removed: cut the Lemons and egg whites into slices, and place them in a glass <term>Cucurbit</term> <note>A gourd-shaped vessel used in early chemistry and distillation to hold the liquid to be heated.</note>, at the bottom of which you have placed one pound of well-washed <term>Venice Turpentine</term> <note>A resin harvested from larch trees, highly valued in early modern medicine for its supposedly purifying and healing properties.</note>; then distill in a boiling <term>Water-bath</term> <note>Also known as a "Bain-marie," this method uses a container of hot water to heat another vessel gently and evenly.</note>, and set the resulting water in the sun. It should be used for washing every evening and morning.

## Of Fennel

The **Essence of Fennel**, taken in five or six drops in half a glass of its distilled water, strengthens the eyesight and expels gas <term>vents: literally "winds," referring to intestinal gas or bloating</term>.

The **water** is good for washing diseased eyes, and for making infusions of Rhubarb and Senna <note>Rhubarb and Senna were frequently used together as a standard laxative or "purgative" treatment.</note>.

The **Tincture** taken in pills expels gas and strengthens the stomach; dilute a piece the size of a hazelnut in a pint <note>original: "chopine," a French unit of liquid measure approximately equal to half a liter or a modern pint.</note> of its water, along with a Scruple <term>Scruple: a traditional unit of apothecary weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</term> of its Salt, and it will be an admirable enema for windy colics.

Its **Salt** is excellent for seasoning the meats of those who suffer from gas.

### CHAPTER III.

*The method of distilling all sorts of Aromatic and tender Simples.*

I have resolved, for the relief of the Reader, to reiterate the method of distilling aromatic <term>Simples|Medicinal herbs or plants used as a single ingredient in a remedy</term> and tender Simples, and the manner of extracting Tinctures and Salts from them. To distill aromatics, it is necessary to provide them with a <term>Menstruum|A liquid solvent used in chemistry and alchemy to dissolve materials or extract their essential properties</term>, especially since an Essence is found within their wood, and wood cannot be easily crushed; furthermore, they do not possess as much moisture as tender Simples. You shall distill them in a copper <term>Alembic|A classic distillation apparatus, often called a "still," consisting of a heated pot and a cooling cap</term> by means of a <term>Refrigeratory|The cooling part of the apparatus, such as a water-cooled condenser, that turns vapor back into liquid</term>, as was stated in the first article regarding distillation.

<summary>This page concludes instructions for a powdered leaf remedy used to clear the head and transitions into a technical guide on distilling aromatic plants using a copper still.</summary>

   For dry herbs, they must be moistened, whether for distillation or to extract the Tincture <note>A tincture is a concentrated herbal extract.</note>. For astringents <note>Substances that shrink or constrict body tissues.</note>, one needs distilled vinegar; for diuretics, white wine; for laxatives, water of borage, bugloss, or chicory; for sudorifics <note>Medicines used to induce sweating.</note>, Blessed Thistle <note>original: "Chardon Benit"; a plant often used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance to treat the plague and digestive issues.</note>. There are "rebellious" bodies which have poisonous qualities that must be penetrated and corrected by spirit of wine <note>Highly concentrated alcohol produced by distilling wine.</note> or aromatic waters.

->Charitable & Easy. 73<-

...ashes, you will have no Salt: it is necessary to burn the <term>simples<note>Simples are individual medicinal herbs used on their own, rather than as part of a complex compound medicine.</note></term> and turn them into white ashes, and extract the Salt by lye which you will make in this way. You will heat water more than lukewarm and pour it over your ashes, so that it sits <term>six fingers<note>A traditional measurement based on the width of a finger; "six fingers" is roughly four to five inches deep.</note></term> above them. Let it cool, then filter it and evaporate the liquid until it reaches the consistency of Salt. If your Salt is not white enough, <term>decrepitate<note>To heat a substance (usually a salt) until it stops crackling, indicating it has lost its internal moisture.</note></term> it in a crucible and redissolve it in its own liquid or common water; filter and dry it, as has been mentioned so many times. Once you are satisfied, by the seventh or eighth time, it will be <term>fusible<note>In alchemy and early chemistry, this meant the substance could be melted into a liquid state without being destroyed.</note></term>.

### Sorrel

The use of this water has the power to cure **catarrhs** <note>Inflammation of the mucous membranes, typically causing an excess of mucus</note> of whatever nature they may be: one must take four ounces in the morning and continue for a month. It must be heated, and a spoonful of white honey dissolved in each dose. One must live on a **diet** <note>A specific nutritional regimen to aid healing</note> during the said time, and **purge** <note>A common medical practice involving laxatives to clear the body of "impurities"</note> oneself two or three times, depending on the location or the part of the body that is ill.

### Blessed Thistle

It is not without reason that this herb carries a "blessing" within its name. Its water is **fortifying** <note>original: "confortative"; restorative or strengthening to the organs</note>; it drives **bad humors** <note>According to the medical theories of the time, "humors" were bodily fluids that caused illness if they became unbalanced or "corrupt"</note> outward through sweat and renews one's strength. One can take from four to six ounces of it. It is good to give to those who have a fever and who have difficulty sweating at the end of their **fit** <note>A period of intense fever, shivering, or illness</note>. Its salt is **sudorific** <note>A substance that promotes sweating to help "break" a fever</note>; one **dram** <note>An apothecary weight, about 3.9 grams or 1/8 of an ounce</note> dissolved in a glass of its water causes abundant sweating.

### Marshmallow

The **water** <note>In this context, "water" refers to a distilled herbal water or hydrosol, a common form of medicine in the 17th century.</note> of the leaves and roots of this herb, in the weight of one ounce, with one ounce of sweet almond oil, drunk in the morning for one month, calms the **burning of urine** <note>Likely referring to symptoms of a urinary tract infection.</note> and expels the <term>sand</term> <note>Historical term for "gravel" or very small kidney/bladder stones.</note> found in the Bladder. ### Pellitory

**Pellitory water** is refreshing when drunk in the weight of two to three ounces; it releases suppressed urine, cures the heat-sores that occur in the mouth, and calms toothache if it is held in the mouth while slightly warm.

# CHAPTER V.

## Method of distilling the Flowers of <term>Simples</term>. <note>In historical medicine, "simples" were individual medicinal plants or herbs used on their own, as opposed to "compounds" which mixed many ingredients.</note>

### Of Roses.

There are several kinds of Roses: red, <note>original: "incarnates"</note> pink, white, and wild roses, called dog roses; all of these have their own particular properties. The method of distilling them is common: everyone uses it as they please; for my part, here is my method.

Take as many Roses as you wish and clean them of all kinds of debris. Pound them in a marble or stone mortar and place them in a well-sealed glass vessel to ferment for eight or ten days in a cellar, until they begin to turn sour. Then, squeeze the said Roses under a press in a very strong cloth bag; filter the resulting liquid and distill it in a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: "au bain" (bain-marie), a technique using a container of water to provide gentle, even heat for delicate substances.</note>, and <term>seal</term> <note>original: "luttez," the act of applying "lute"—a specialized airtight paste—to the joints of the distilling apparatus to prevent the escape of medicinal vapors.</note> all the...

<summary>This page introduces the distillation of medicinal flowers, specifically categorizing different types of roses and detailing a method for fermenting and pressing them before distillation.</summary>
<keywords>distillation, roses, simples, fermentation, medicinal herbs, water bath, dog rose, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for distilling rose-water and tinctures, likely following a description of the initial preparation of the rose petals.</meta>

->88               **Chemistry**<-

Seal the joints of your vessels, and start with a low heat at the beginning; for the first water that rises is only <term>phlegm</term> <note>In early chemistry, "phlegm" refers to the watery, tasteless part of a substance that distills off first, considered to have little medicinal value.</note>, and is not fragrant, whereas that which comes in the middle certainly is. Set the first, second, and third parts aside, and you will see that the second has more strength and scent. As for what remains at the bottom of your vessel, with the consistency of syrup, it is a <term>Tincture</term> <note>A concentrated liquid extract of a medicinal substance.</note>, which has the same properties as syrup of roses. It keeps for several years without spoiling, even though it is made without sugar. I have tested many times that it was more <term>purgative</term> <note>A substance used to "purge" the bowels; a laxative.</note> than the common syrup. As for your rose-water, you may <term>rectify</term> <note>To purify or concentrate a liquid by distilling it again.</note> it over fresh rose juice, and let it <term>circulate</term> <note>A process where vapors are condensed and returned to the boiling liquid to increase its potency.</note>, like spirit of wine, to make it more <term>spiritual</term> <note>Refined, volatile, and containing the essential "spirit" or essence of the plant.</note>.

Take the <term>marc</term> <note>The solid remains—petals and stems—left after the juice or essence has been pressed out.</note> of the aforementioned roses, and put it back into the glass vessel; pour common water over it until it floats two fingers' breadth above, then seal it and let it ferment in the cellar for fifteen days: then press it out, filter it, and distill it as before, and you will have rose-water even better than—

<summary>This page provides practical instructions for distilling roses to create rose-water and a medicinal tincture, emphasizing the separation of the "spirit" from the "phlegm." It also describes a secondary process of fermenting the leftover solids (marc) to produce an even higher quality rose-water.</summary>

## Clear Violet Water

**TAKE** some <term>spirit of wine (a high-proof alcohol or brandy)</term>, into which you will place violet flowers to infuse—ensuring they are well-trimmed <note>original: "épluchées" — meaning the green stems and sepals have been removed to leave only the petals</note> and clean—inside a glass vessel. Let the spirit of wine float four fingers' breadth above the flowers. Infuse this in a <term>water bath (bain-marie)</term> until the flowers turn white. Your spirit will then be red; filter it through a <term>hippocras bag (a conical cloth filter used for clarifying liquids)</term>, and pour it back over fresh flowers, just as you did before. You will also add a small linen bag containing crushed cinnamon. Upon this second infusion, your spirit will turn violet; filter it again as you did the first time. Dissolve as much sugar as you like in rose water, then combine it with your spirit. Expose this mixture to the sun for as long as is possible. This water has the power to comfort the stomach, to help clear phlegm, and to soothe a cough. Its dose is one spoonful.

<summary>A recipe for a medicinal violet-infused liqueur or "water" designed to treat stomach issues and respiratory congestion. It involves a double-infusion process to achieve a specific violet color and uses a water bath and sun-aging for preparation.</summary>

96      ***Chemistry***

### Water Lily Flowers.
The water of water lily flowers cools; it induces sleep; it is good for the heat of fever, taking two spoonfuls mixed into a glass of tisane <term>tisane: a medicinal herbal tea or barley water</term>. It stops dysentery and "white flowers" <note>original: "fleurs blanches" – a historical medical term for leucorrhea or vaginal discharge</note>, when four ounces are drunk for three consecutive mornings. It soothes gout that arises from hot causes <note>Humoral medicine categorized illnesses like gout as "hot" or "cold" depending on the perceived imbalance of bodily fluids</note> and relieves headaches.

Its tincture, at the consistency of an extract, mixed with wax, makes a plaster for inflammations; it cools the affected parts and repels humors <term>repels humors: a treatment method known as "repercussion," intended to drive excess fluids away from an inflamed area</term>.

A honey is made from its flowers for cooling enemas, which is prepared in the same manner as violet honey <note>original: "violat" – a medicinal preparation of honey or syrup infused with violets</note>.

### Borage and Bugloss Flowers.
The waters distilled from flowers have something more spiritual and delicate about them than those extracted from the leaves, stems, and roots. Those made from the flowers of Borage <term>Borage: *Borago officinalis*, traditionally used for its cooling and "cheering" properties</term> and Bugloss <term>Bugloss: *Anchusa officinalis*, a plant closely related to borage used to treat fevers</term> are better than

### Orange Flowers.

YOU shall put your orange flowers, without any other preparation, into a glass gourd <term>cucurbit</term><note>A gourd-shaped vessel used as the base of a still</note>, and press them down a little so that they do not take up so much space. Lute <note>To seal the joints of the apparatus with a paste or cement to prevent vapors from escaping</note> the cap and the receiver well, for fear that the spirits <note>The volatile essences or medicinal vapors</note> might be lost: distill in a boiling bath <term>bain-marie</term>, until nothing more rises. Extract the salt from the mass <note>The solid residue remaining after distillation</note>, and revive your water, and expose it to the sun, stoppered with a piece of pierced parchment, as has been previously described.

This water stops the suffocations that arise from the womb <note>Historically referred to as "the vapors" or "fits of the mother," a condition where the uterus was believed to put pressure on other organs</note>. One may take from one to three ounces at the onset of the fit. Furthermore, it induces vomiting without effort.

### Alkekengi Fruits

### Tincture of Saffron.

You will extract this Tincture like the previous one, with spirit of wine. It is a friend to the heart and the lungs. It has a great familiarity with all the other internal organs, and particularly with the womb <term>matrice: an archaic term for the uterus</term>.

It opens, digests, and softens; it soothes pains, induces sleep, provokes the months <note>original: "les mois," a common historical euphemism for the menstrual cycle</note>, and helps to bring forth the child <note>referring to its use in facilitating labor and childbirth</note>. The use of this Tincture is very frequent for apoplexy <term>apoplexy: a historical term for a sudden loss of consciousness or a stroke</term>, vapors of the womb <term>vapors of the womb: a common diagnosis for various physical and emotional symptoms believed to originate in the uterus</term>, jaundice, asthma, and in all venomous and malignant diseases. The dose is five to six drops in some suitable water.

### Virtues of the Essence of the Clove Bud

The **ESSENCE** of Clove strengthens a weakened nature, whether from being overburdened by excesses of drinking or eating, or from having suffered by being given no nourishment at all. It works through the digestion of the former, and restores the strength and natural heat, and gives vigor which would be weakened by the lack of the latter. People of all ages can fall into these two extremes: that is why it is good for the old and the young, when they lack natural heat, and for all <term>cold diseases<note>In the "humoral" medicine of the time, illnesses were classified as hot, cold, moist, or dry. Clove, being "hot," was used to balance "cold" ailments like indigestion or sluggishness.</note></term>. The dose is three to four drops in wine, or in the water of **Bugloss**, **Borage**, **Lemon Balm**, **Blessed Thistle**, or in its own distilled water. It strengthens chilled limbs and revives them. ### Of Nutmeg.

**Nutmeg** is not distilled at all; its
spirits are condensed together, &
have no ease for rising <note>Early modern chemists believed nutmeg's active components were too "heavy" or "oily" to vaporize and rise through a distillation neck.</note>.
What is called essence of Nutmeg
is rather a tincture than an es-
sence. It is extracted in this way:
Slice your Nutmeg into sections,
& place it in a glass <term>cucurbit<note>The lower, gourd-shaped part of a still where the ingredients are heated.</note></term>,
& pour spirit of wine <note>High-proof alcohol, similar to modern brandy or ethanol.</note> over it:
then put on a <term>blind head<note>original: "Chapiteau aveugle." A still-head without a spout, used to reflect vapors back down into the vessel rather than collecting them elsewhere.</note></term>, &
let it digest in a <term>water bath<note>original: "Bain." Refers to a "bain-marie," used for gentle, indirect heating.</note></term> at a lukewarm heat, un-
til your spirit becomes colored.
Then pour it off by <term>decantation<note>original: "inclination." The process of slowly tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the sediment behind.</note></term>, & re-
place it with more spirit over the <term>dregs<note>original: "feces." The solid residue or sediment remaining after the liquid has been extracted.</note></term> re-
maining at the bottom of the vessel. Repeat
until your spirit no longer draws
any tincture. Take all your
tinctures, & recover your spirit by
distillation in the water bath; the essence, or ra-
ther the tincture, will remain at the bottom.

## CHAPTER IX.

->*Of Gums & Resins.*<-

->*Turpentine.*<-

**A**ll Gums & Resins are distilled *by descent* <note>original: *per Descensum*; a distillation method where the heat is applied from above and the distilled liquid flows downward through a filter or grate</note>, as is stated in its proper place. From some of these, one extracts the tincture in the manner I have described regarding the extraction of Tinctures. Of all the resinous Gums, only Turpentine alone is distilled by the Retort <term>Retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-curved neck used for distilling substances</term> in this manner. Take Venice Turpentine; the whitest is the best, and place it in a retort; pour water over it, until it sits two fingers' depth above it; let your vessel, at most, be filled only halfway. Attach another retort <note>functioning here as a receiver to collect the liquid</note>, and seal the joints well with lute <term>lute: a paste or cement used to make laboratory seals airtight</term>: distill with a gentle fire, for fear that the material might swell <note>meaning to foam up or boil over</note>: continue your operation until you see a red oil rise—

Then change your **Receiver** <note>original: "Recipient" — the glass vessel used in distillation to collect the condensing vapors.</note>, and increase your fire until nothing more rises into your first Receiver; you will have a water and an **essence** that you will separate with a funnel; the essence will remain on top. As for the **red oil**, which came out last, it is a balm for fresh wounds: it heals them, and it softens and stretches nerves that have become shortened or contracted by cold. It aids digestion when applied to the stomach.

*Of Myrrh & Frankincense.*

### Virtues of the oil & bark of Ash.

## CHAPTER II.

### Of the magistery of the Human Skull.

## CHAPTER III.

*Concerning the Admirable Oil of Human Bones.*

TAKE the bones of a man, the largest ones you can find; break them, and heat them in the fire until they are glowing red: and when they are red, you shall place them in a glazed earthenware pot in which you have put a sufficient quantity of *sain* <term>sain: an old term for rendered fat or lard</term>, or human fat: cover the pot and let them soak, then remove them from within, crush them, and place them in a retort <term>retort: a spherical glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used for distilling substances</term> along with the fat that remained in the pot: distill using a sand bath <note>A "sand bath" (feu de sable) involves placing the vessel in a container of sand which is then heated, allowing for a more even and controlled temperature than a direct flame.</note>, and continue your distillation until nothing more rises; expose this liquid to the sun. This is a specific <note>In early medicine, a "specific" was a remedy believed to have a unique power to cure a particular disease or part of the body.</note> for all pains of the nerves and joints, and for sciatica.

<summary>This page provides the final dosage instructions for a rose remedy and details a macabre alchemical process for distilling "admirable oil" from charred human bones and fat.</summary>

# CHAPTER IV.

## On Meat Waters.

### On Capon Water.

If you wish to distill a Capon, or Chicken, you shall skin it while it is still alive <note>This was a common, though harsh, practice in early modern medicine; it was believed that skinning the animal while "warm" or alive preserved the "vital spirits" or medicinal essence that would otherwise be lost after death.</note>, and remove its feet, head, and all the entrails. You shall crush its bones in a stone mortar with a wooden pestle, and put it in pieces into a glass <term>cucurbit</term> <note>The cucurbit is the bottom part of a distilling apparatus, usually shaped like a gourd, where the substance to be distilled is placed.</note>, with a handful of pearl barley. Pour over it a pint of bugloss or borage water <note>Bugloss and borage were "cordial" herbs, thought to gladden the heart and cool the blood.</note>, and distill it in a boiling bath <note>A "Bain-marie," or a vessel of boiling water in which the glass flask is placed to ensure gentle, indirect heat.</note>, so that the spirits of the Capon rise with the water; draw off a pint of liquor, then cease your operation.

This water is restorative and <term>pectoral</term> <note>A term for medicines intended to treat the chest, lungs, and respiratory system.</note>. It renews the strength of persons debilitated by illness, by taking four or five spoonfuls, five or six times a day in this manner. One may distill all kinds of meats, and there

## CHAPTER VII.

### *Of the Spirit, Oil & Tincture of Honey.*

MIX one pound of very clean sand with two pounds of skimmed honey, and put them into a retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note>, and distill over a sand bath: a white water will come out first; and when a red liquor begins to rise, change the receiver <note>the vessel used to collect the distilled liquid</note>: this is the spirit of honey, which must not be mixed <note>referring to keeping the red spirit separate from the initial white water</note>. When you see that nothing more will distill, increase your fire, and a thick oil will come out, which also must not be mixed with the spirit. Rectify <note>the process of purifying a liquid by repeated distillation</note> the spirit in a water bath <note>a vessel of hot water used to heat another container gently; also called a bain-marie</note>; the tincture will remain at the bottom, and the spirit will distill like water.

The virtues of the spirit of honey are very great: if they were known, its use would be more frequent than it is. It protects the body from all putrefaction <note>the process of decay or rotting</note>, and preserves health for a very long time. Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), a Roman author and natural philosopher known for his 'Natural History'</note> writes that an aged soldier

## CHAPTER IX.

### On the Oil of Butter.

This must be applied three times a day.

# CHAPTER X.

## *On the Oil of Hair.*

TAKE some Hair, and make small balls of it, and place them in a Retort <term>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</term>, which you shall fill at most three-quarters full, and attach a Receiver to the Retort, which you will lute <term>seal the connection between the two vessels with an airtight paste</term>: then distill over a circular fire <note>original: "feu de roüe"; a method of heating where the fuel is placed in a circle around the vessel to provide even heat</note> until no more smoke emerges.

This oil has a very penetrating odor. It is good against suffocations of the womb <note>a historical medical diagnosis for various physical and emotional symptoms believed to be caused by a "wandering" or "stifled" uterus</note>: one must rub it on the temples and the nostrils. It also makes hair grow, if one applies it for eight or ten consecutive days to the spot where one wishes it to grow. Before applying it, the head must be well rubbed with a slightly warm cloth.

### CHAPTER XI.

### On the Spirit of Milk.

**BRING** milk to a boil; pour into it a spoonful of vinegar, then filter the said milk, and place it in a glass **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the base of a still</term>, and distill it in a boiling water-bath <note>known as a *Bain-Marie*</note>, and continue until nothing more rises. Remove the dregs <note>original: *feces*; the solid residue remaining after the liquid has evaporated</note> that are in the cucurbit, and **rectify** <term>rectify: to purify a liquid, especially a spirit, by repeating the distillation process</term> your spirit. Lower your fire by one degree, and from the four pints you have, draw off only three pints and a **chopin** <note>a *choppine* was an old French unit of volume, roughly equal to half a liter or one pint</note>: remove the dregs, and rectify once more, and distill over a lukewarm fire, drawing off only three pints.

The use of this spirit is healthier and more refreshing than common **whey** <note>the liquid remaining after milk has been curdled and strained</note>; its "coldness" <note>In the medical theory of the time, milk was classified as having a "cold" temperament; the heat of distillation was believed to balance this</note> is corrected by the fire, and the harmful qualities are separated. It is not at all harmful to the stomach: one may add sugar of roses or sugar of violets, depending on the patient <note>original: *ſujet*</note>.

## Concerning Minerals and Metals.

### FOREWORD.

**A**LTHOUGH Metals and Minerals seem to be distant from man, and although Holy Scripture <note>The author is likely referring to the account of the six days of Creation in Genesis, which explicitly mentions plants and animals but not the specific formation of ores.</note> makes no mention of their creation, they nevertheless do not fail to provide us with very <term>salutary<note>From the Latin 'salutaris,' meaning health-giving or life-saving.</note></term> remedies. Today, Medicine uses them with happy success. It is necessary that their preparations be exactly made, insofar as these are <term>violent remedies<note>In 17th-century medical contexts, "violent" refers to substances that are chemically potent or "aggressive" (such as antimony or mercury) and can be toxic if not refined correctly.</note></term>; although they are only taken in small quantities, and for stubborn and <term>inveterate<note>Long-standing, chronic, or deeply rooted in the body.</note></term> illnesses. That is why I am omitting from this section the operations performed on gold and on silver, as I am not familiar with their preparations or their usefulness in medicine. I have seen several operations which were given the names **Potable Gold** <note>original: "Or potable"; a legendary alchemical elixir made of dissolved gold, believed to have life-prolonging and curative properties</note>, **tincture of gold**, and **oil of silver**, which I have not been able to understand; I cannot convince myself that bodies so "perfect" and condensed <note>In 17th-century chemistry, "perfect metals" like gold and silver were considered pure and extremely resistant to change or decomposition</note> could be made liquid. It is not that I condemn these operations simply because I cannot conceive of them; I would be as reckless as a blind person who claimed the Sun did not exist just because they could not see it. As for the operations that follow, I guarantee that they are true and have been proven through experience.

The dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid residue left in a vessel after distillation or filtration</note> remaining from your first distillation shall be burned to extract the salt from them, using the phlegm <term>phlegm: in early chemistry, the watery, tasteless distillate that lacks active medicinal properties</term> that you previously distilled, or with a bit of warm common water. This salt is extracted in the same manner as that of botanical matter.

The virtues <note>The "virtues" of a substance refer to its inherent medicinal powers or effective properties</note> of the spirit of Vitriol <term>spirit of Vitriol: a dilute form of sulfuric acid, often produced by distilling green vitriol (iron sulfate)</term> are great. It should be noted that it is never taken alone, and that its dose must never exceed three to four drops, taken in some vehicle <note>A "vehicle" is a liquid like wine, water, or syrup used to dilute a potent medicine for safer ingestion</note> suitable for the ailment. It tempers the heat of malignant and violent fevers and consumes the putrefaction of the humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to determine a person's health and temperament</term> by which they are caused. It purifies the blood and penetrates even into the veins; it is diuretic; it kills worms; applied with a pledget <term>pledget: a small wad or compress of lint or cotton used to apply ointments or liquids to a wound</term> it removes chancres <note>Sores or small ulcers</note> and heals ulcers of the mouth. Care must be taken that it does not touch any part other than the sore, as it corrodes the flesh; it whitens the teeth if they are rubbed with a small cloth; it helps to extract the tinctures <note>The concentrated colors or medicinal essences extracted from plants</note> of all kinds of flowers.

The oil of Vitriol <term>oil of Vitriol: a more concentrated and corrosive form of sulfuric acid than the "spirit"</term>, which remained previously—

### Of the Spirit of Sea Salt.

This spirit is extracted in two ways; one by distillation, the other by dissolution and deliquescence. <note>Deliquescence is the process where a substance absorbs moisture from the air until it dissolves and becomes a liquid.</note> For both of these, it is necessary to decrepitate the salt, <note>Decrepitation is the process of heating salt until it stops making a crackling or popping sound.</note> as was said in the Chapter on Operations. Take your decrepitated salt and grind it into a very fine powder: if you wish to distill it, mix two pounds of salt with one pound of brick powder, or fragments of the pot in which you decrepitated it: put the whole mixture into a <term>Retort</term> <note>A glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling chemicals.</note>, and attach a <term>Receiver</term> to it, in which you will put one pound of water. <term>Lute</term> <note>To "lute" means to seal the joints between the vessels with a paste or clay to prevent vapors from escaping.</note> and distill as you did for the spirit of Vitriol. <note>Spirit of Vitriol is a historical name for sulfuric acid; here it serves as a reference for the distillation procedure.</note> Your operation being done, unseal your vessels, and put what remains in the Receiver into a glass <term>Cucurbit</term> <note>A gourd-shaped vessel that forms the lower part of a still.</note>, and draw off the water that you put in <unclear>previously</unclear>

Many <term>Philosophers<note>The term "Philosopher" here specifically refers to Alchemists or Natural Philosophers.</note></term> assert that its use is capable of regenerating man.

## CHAPTER IV.

### Of Sulfur.

#### Of the spirit of Sulfur.

**TAKE** a glass bell-jar <note>original: "Campane de verre." These were common garden tools used to protect young plants from frost.</note>, of the kind used to cover **Melons**; suspend it by its knob beneath a chimney-piece: place beneath it a small earthenware pan, in which you will put spongy **Beef bones**, burned and cleared of their outer coating; they are called the **perpetual wick** <note>The porous, calcined bone acts as a heat-resistant platform that helps the sulfur burn steadily without being consumed itself.</note>: arrange upon them a pound of **Sulfur in rolls** <term>Roll sulfur (soufre en canon): sulfur cast into cylinders or "canons," which was the standard commercial form for high-quality sulfur.</term>, which you will set on fire with a red-hot iron, or a lighted candle. Tilt the bell-jar to one side to give passage to the liquor <term>Spirit of sulfur: an early name for dilute sulfuric acid, produced here by collecting the condensation of sulfurous vapors.</term> that will form inside, so that it falls easily into a vessel that you will place beneath. This operation is performed without the **Artist** <note>the chemist or apothecary performing the work</note> being required to remain present: all the **Sulfur** must be allowed to burn before adding more.    This spirit has an infinite number of properties: Taken in pure Chervil water, it purifies the blood. It induces sweating when taken in Blessed Thistle water; it cleanses the lungs in Hyssop water; it cures daily fever <note>original: "fiévre quotidiéne"; a fever that recurs every day, often associated with malaria.</note> in Rosemary water; tertian fever <note>original: "fiévre tierce"; a fever that recurs every three days.</note> in Centaury water; quartan fever <note>original: "fiévre quarte"; a fever that recurs every four days.</note> in Bugloss water. Its dose is from five to six drops. It is suitable for ulcers of the mouth; it whitens teeth; it serves to extract the tinctures of Roses and Violets, just like the spirit of Vitriol.

# TO MADEMOISELLE MEVRDRAC, <note>Marie Meurdrac (c. 1610–1680) was a pioneering French chemist. Her book, published in 1666, was one of the first chemistry and "beauty-science" manuals written by a woman for women.</note>
## On the dedication of her Book.

### SIX-LINE STANZA <note>original: "SIXAIN"—a French poetic form consisting of a single stanza of six lines.</note>

-> Though you might have, through your Works, <-
-> Obtained every advantage <-
-> Justly due to the fruits of your mind, <-
-> You would never have received such high praise <-
-> As when you dedicated this writing <-
-> To her in whom one sees the qualities of an Angel. <note>It was customary to dedicate books to noble patrons to gain social standing and financial support. This "Angel" likely refers to the Comtesse de Guiche, to whom Meurdrac dedicated her work, though the name of the poem's author, Angélique, adds a clever wordplay.</note>

ANGÉLIQUE SALERNE.

<summary>This page features a short poem of praise written by Angélique Salerne to honor the chemist Marie Meurdrac. It celebrates Meurdrac's intellectual achievements and her choice of a virtuous patron for her book's dedication.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, dedication, women in science, 17th-century poetry, chemistry, Angélique Salerne, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page serves as a dedicatory sonnet for the work of Marie Meurdrac, a 17th-century chemist. It establishes the theme of the book: making the "secrets of nature" accessible to everyone rather than keeping them hidden in the obscure language of traditional alchemy.</meta>

[Decorative border of fleur-de-lis ornaments]

->TO<-
->**MADEMOISELLE MEVRDRAC,**<-
->Regarding her *Charitable and Easy Chemistry*<- <note>Marie Meurdrac (c. 1610–1680) was a French chemist and alchemist who published her treatise specifically to provide medical and cosmetic recipes to those who could not afford expensive doctors.</note>

->SONNET.<-

Stop, O people, stop building Altars
To those who had only given you a mere glimpse <note>original: "peinture," literally a painting or a sketch, implying a vague or superficial representation.</note>
Of the secrets that Nature kept within her breast,
And no longer place them among the Immortals.

No, no, it was wrong to hold them in such regard,
Since they veiled from us in a pitch-black night
What God had placed within every creature
For the common use of all other mortals. <note>The author is criticizing earlier male alchemists for using "Hermetic" or coded language to hide their knowledge from the public.</note>

We owe to you, **MEVRDRAC**, far more the honors
That the Ancients once paid to their Sages;
They hid their art from us with too much studied care,

But you, you show us it is no longer difficult,
And through a spirit filled with generosity
Finally you make it charitable and easy for us.

P. D. L.

ã v <note>These characters are a printer's mark, or signature, used to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>A laudatory sonnet praising Marie Meurdrac for demystifying chemistry and sharing medical knowledge for the common good, contrasting her transparency with the secrecy of ancient sages.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, sonnet, dedication, history of chemistry, female scientists, alchemy, accessibility of knowledge, charitable medicine</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page contains a dedicatory poem (sonnet) from the preliminary matter of Marie Meurdrac’s "La Chymie Charitable et Facile" (Easy and Charitable Chemistry), first published in 1666. It serves to establish the author's authority by linking her work to Apollo, the god of medicine.</meta>

[Decorative border of floral ornaments]

->### APOLLO’S PROSOPOPOEIA <note>A literary device in which an imagined person or a personified deity—in this case, the god Apollo—is represented as speaking.</note><-
->### Regarding the Work of Mademoiselle<-
->### MEURDRAC.<-

->#### SONNET.<-

Medicine comes from my invention,
And I am, after God, the Operator of the world:
My power has no equal anywhere,
And all things are born from my action alone.

Herbs and fruits are my production:
Animals in the air, on the earth, and in the wave <note>original: "l'onde"; a poetic reference to the sea or water.</note>:
I make minerals by my fertile virtue,
And the **King of metals** <note>In the 17th century, "the king of metals" was the standard alchemical and chemical term for gold.</note> in its perfection.

I have among Mortals two women as Companions,
Of whom the illustrious **de GUICHE** <note>Likely referring to the Duchess of Guiche, a prominent noblewoman of the period who may have been a patron or an inspiration for the work.</note>, by stealing my flames,
Shines in the Court like another Sun:

And the learned **MEURDRAC** in her style of writing,
By imitating my Art <note>Apollo is the god of both the arts and medicine/healing.</note> shares my Empire:
And not one of the three of us finds an equal.

->I. D. S. N. <note>These initials likely belong to the poet who composed this tribute to Meurdrac.</note><-

<summary>This page features a celebratory sonnet written from the perspective of the god Apollo, who praises Marie Meurdrac for her mastery of chemistry and medicine. The poem elevates her work by placing her alongside the gods and high nobility, asserting that her scientific writing shares in Apollo's own divine "empire" over nature.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, Apollo, sonnet, 17th-century chemistry, women in science, gold, alchemy, medicine, Duchess of Guiche</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page contains a celebratory dedicatory poem (sonnet) addressed to Marie Meurdrac, the author of a 17th-century chemistry manual. This follows the frontispiece or title page of the work.</meta>

[Decorative border of floral and ornamental motifs]

->To **MISS MEVRDRAC**,<note>Marie Meurdrac (c. 1610–1680) was a French chemist and alchemist. Her work, *La Chymie Charitable et Facile, en faveur des Dames* (Easy and Charitable Chemistry for the Benefit of Ladies), was one of the first chemistry texts written by a woman.</note><-
->On her Book of Chemistry.<-

->**SONNET.**<-

A hundred times I have celebrated the most sublime deeds
Of the distinguished Heroes that History boasts of:
But when I must speak of an illustrious learned woman,
I either speak with trembling, **MEVRDRAC**, or I keep silent.

Your book shows us marvelous effects,
Which have surpassed the expectations of the most envious;
Whether your mind imitates nature, or whether it invents,
The curious person <note>In the 17th century, "the curious" (les curieux) referred to students of science, collectors, and those seeking new knowledge.</note> sees there the fulfillment of their wishes.

How much glory our descendants <note>original: "Neveux," literally nephews, but used here in the classical sense to mean future generations or posterity.</note> will one day give you:
And to tell the truth, it will be difficult to believe
That a woman ever possessed so much knowledge.

Of the secrets that your Book explains on every
page,
To hide nothing from you, I would wish only to have
The secret of worthily praising your Work.

->**DU PELLETIER.**<-
ã vj <note>A signature mark used by early modern printers to guide the binder in assembling the book's pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>A laudatory sonnet by Du Pelletier praising Marie Meurdrac for her scientific expertise and the "marvelous effects" described in her chemistry book. The poem highlights the rarity and impressive nature of a woman possessing such extensive scientific knowledge in the 17th century.</summary>
<keywords>Marie Meurdrac, Du Pelletier, women in science, 17th-century chemistry, sonnet, scientific secrets, female education</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page contains a celebratory sonnet, likely serving as a preface to a printed book on cosmetics or medicine. It establishes a tone of high praise for the author and the book's patroness.</meta>

->### S O N N E T.<-

->Tell us what Spirit, or what Deity,<-
->Inspires the knowledge seen in your **Work**,<-
->And who has given you this fine art as your share<-
->To prolong days, and to restore health?<-

->You know, you say, how to preserve beauty,<-
->And how to repair the damage to one's complexion;<-
->But I promise you the homage of the *gallant sex* <note>original: "Sexe galand." In the 17th and 18th centuries, this phrase was a polite way to refer to women in high society.</note>,<-
->If you have truly found this longed-for secret.<-

->Not that they hope to possess that delicacy<-
->Which is seen shining in the complexion of the lovely **Countess**<-
->To whom you dedicate this perfect **Work**.<-

->The vainest among us wishes herself less beautiful,<-
->And your finest secrets will have effect enough<-
->If they allow us to follow, from afar, this illustrious **Model**.<-

->**MISS D. I.** <note>The signature "M.lle D. I." likely refers to a female contemporary or friend of the author who provided this complimentary poem.</note><-

<summary>A laudatory sonnet praising the author's expertise in medicine and beauty, while paying tribute to the aristocratic woman to whom the book is dedicated.</summary>
<keywords>sonnet, poetry, cosmetics, health, beauty, dedication, 17th-century style, Countess</keywords>

<term>Rectification</term>, 19 <note>The process of purifying a liquid, typically through repeated distillation to increase its strength.</note>
<term>Calcination</term>, 20 <note>Heating a solid to high temperatures in the presence of air to remove volatile parts or to oxidize it.</note>
<term>Cohobation</term>, 20 <note>A specialized distillation where the liquid product is poured back over the solid residue and re-distilled multiple times.</note>
Coagulation, 21
Filtration, 22
Desiccation, 22 <note>The thorough drying of a substance.</note>
<term>Dulcoration</term>, 22 <note>Literally "sweetening"; the process of washing a substance to remove acidic or salty impurities.</note>
<term>Inclination</term>, 23 <note>Gently pouring off a liquid to separate it from its sediment; modern decanting.</note>
<term>Amalgamation</term>, 23 <note>The process of mixing a metal (often gold or silver) with mercury.</note>
<term>Digestion</term>, 23 <note>Applying gentle, consistent heat to a substance over a long period, similar to incubation.</note>
<term>Putrefaction</term>, 24 <note>The intentional "rotting" or decomposition of a substance through gentle heat, seen as a necessary stage of transformation.</note>
<term>Menstruum</term>, 25 <note>Original: "Menstruë"; a solvent used to dissolve solids, named for the "monthly" (lunar) cycles of time it often took to work.</note>
Fermentation, 25
<term>Circulation</term>, 26 <note>A continuous distillation process where vapors condense and fall back into the boiling liquid in a closed vessel.</note>
<term>Deliquescence</term>, 27 <note>Original: "Defaillance"; the process where a substance absorbs moisture from the air until it dissolves into a liquid.</note>
<term>Reverberation</term>, 27 <note>Heating in a furnace where the flames are reflected or "reverberated" off the roof onto the material.</note>
Precipitation, 27
<term>Brine</term>, 28 <note>Original: "Eau marinée"; likely referring to saltwater or a salted solution used for chemical baths.</note>
<term>Stratification</term>, 28 <note>Placing different substances in alternating layers within a crucible or vessel.</note>
<term>Torrefaction</term>, 29 <note>Roasting or parching a substance over a fire to dry it or make it brittle.</note>
<term>Decrepitation</term>, 30 <note>The crackling or popping sound made by certain salts when heated as their water of crystallization escapes.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the legal and medical certification for Marie Meurdrac’s chemistry manual, typically found at the beginning or end of 17th-century French books to prove their legitimacy and safety.</meta>

*Extract of the King's Privilege.* <note>A "Privilege" was a precursor to modern copyright, where the King granted an author or printer the exclusive right to publish a specific work for a set number of years.</note>

BY grace & Privilege of the King, it is per-
mitted to Mademoiselle MARIE MEURDRAC
to have printed a Book she has composed,
entitled *Easy and Charitable Chemistry, etc.* <note>original: "La Chymie Charitable & facile"</note>
for the duration of ten years: with prohibitions
to all others to print, sell, or dis-
tribute it during the said time, without the consent of
the said Petitioner or of those who shall hold rights
from her, under the penalties set forth more fully
by the said Privilege. Given at Paris the 20th of De-
cember 1665. Signed, DE QUIGY.

And the said Mademoiselle Marie Meurdrac has ceded and
transferred her right of Privilege for the time and under the
clauses it contains, to Jean d’Houry, Merchant
Bookseller, according to the agreement made between them.

*Registered in the Book of the Community <note>The Guild of Printers and Booksellers</note>, the 5th of Jan-*
*uary 1666. Signed,* PIGET.

___

*Approval of the Doctors of Medicine.*

WE the undersigned Doctors-Regent <term>Doctor-Regent: A senior member of the medical faculty who held the right to teach and vote on university matters</term> of the
Faculty of Medicine in Paris, certify
to have seen and read a Book entitled *Easy*
*and Charitable Chemistry*: Composed by Mademoiselle
MARIE MEUDRAC <note>The original text omits the first 'r' in her name here</note> in which we find
nothing that might not be useful to the Public. In
witness whereof we have signed. Done this 10th of
December 1665.
Signed, LE VIGNON, Dean.
DE CAEN. DE BOURGES.

-># Foreword<-

...to preserve, but also to increase
the advantages they have received from
Nature; that it is of great interest, and that it
teaches faithfully and familiarly how to

be to sin against Charity <note>In this context, "Charity" refers to the Christian duty of selfless love and the sharing of knowledge for the common good.</note> to hide the
knowledge that God has given me,
which can benefit everyone.
This is the sole motive that made me resolve
to let this book leave my hands:
I hope the public will be grateful to me for it,
and that they will not stop so much to critique
the elegance of my style—which the subject
that I treat could not allow—as to
profit from my precepts, in order to
succeed well, and to become exact in the oper-
ations <note>Technical or medicinal procedures; the term "operations" suggests a practical, perhaps chemical or pharmaceutical, manual.</note> that they will take the trouble to
practice. I further ask this
favor of those who wish to under-
take them, that they distribute liberally
to the poor the remedies just as I have
done until now, since I am teaching them
the means to make them almost
without expense; and since it is just, fi-
nally, that I profit from my late-night labors <note>original: "veilles" — the hours spent working or studying while others sleep.</note>, I
beseech them, as my only reward,
to remember me in their charities <note>The author is asking for the reader's prayers or for acts of mercy to be performed in his name as a form of spiritual payment.</note>

<summary>The author concludes his preface by explaining that he is publishing his knowledge as a religious duty to help others, especially the poor. <meta>This page continues a sentence from the previous page, where the author expresses her hope for the readers' success in their chemical or medicinal endeavors.</meta>

->*Foreword.*<-

...that they will perform, and to make me a participant in the merit <note>In a religious context, "merit" refers to the spiritual credit gained from doing good deeds</note> of their good works; obtaining for me from Heaven through their prayers, and through those of the poor whom they will relieve, new lights <note>meaning new insights or divine inspirations</note>, and even more useful knowledge that I may once again communicate to them. As for the Ladies who will be content simply to know, without wishing to take the trouble to perform the operations they judge necessary for themselves—due to the time required, and the various types of vessels <note>glassware or containers used in a laboratory</note> and other utensils needed, or who fear they may not succeed—I will explain myself in person when I am honored with a communication, and I will take care to perform myself whatever may be desired of what I teach. I have divided this Book into six **Parts**: in the **first**, I treat the principles and operations, vessels, lutes <note>sealants used to make laboratory apparatus airtight</note>, furnaces, fires, characters <note>symbolic shorthand used in chemistry and alchemy</note>, and weights; in the **second**, I speak of the virtue <note>the medicinal properties or "power"</note> of **Simples** <note>individual medicinal herbs or plants used as remedies</note>, of their preparations, and of the manner of extracting the...

### CHAPTER ONE.
#### On Salt.

**C**hemistry has as its object **mixed bodies** <term>In early science, "mixed bodies" referred to any substance composed of various elements combined together, such as plants, minerals, or animal matter.</term> insofar as they are divisible and capable of being broken down. Chemistry works upon these bodies to extract from them the **three Principles**, which are **Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury** <note>These three substances, known as the *Tria Prima*, were considered by followers of Paracelsus to be the fundamental building blocks of all physical matter.</note>; this is achieved through two general operations: namely, **Solution** <note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid.</note> and **Congelation** <note>The process of thickening or solidifying a substance.</note>. ### CHAPTER II.
*Of Sulfur.*

**SULFUR**, the second principle, creates the union between the spirit and the body; this is why it is called the <term>Soul</term> <note>In the 'Tria Prima' theory of Paracelsus, the Soul (Sulfur) mediates between the Spirit (Mercury) and the Body (Salt).</note> by some authors. It is of three kinds: coarse, medium, and subtle. The coarse joins with the <term>Fixed Salt</term> <note>The non-volatile residue left after calcination</note>, the medium with the <term>Salt of Nitre</term> <note>Commonly known as saltpeter or potassium nitrate</note>, and the subtle with the <term>Ammoniac</term> <note>Sal ammoniac or ammonium chloride, known for its volatility</note>. 

The coarse sulfur is present in small quantities, and yet it is what gives the <term>balsamic virtue</term> <note>The healing, soothing, and preservative properties</note> to the Fixed Salt.

The medium sulfur is composed of fatty, hot, and oily parts; it burns easily, is visible through its effects, dominates the Salt of Nitre, and joins with it.

4 *Chemistry*

Subtle Sulfur joins with <term>Sal Ammoniac<note>original: "Sel Armoniac"; ammonium chloride, a volatile salt used in alchemical processes to help substances "rise" or sublimate when heated.</note></term>; it is this sulfur which facilitates its elevation and causes the spirits to catch fire.

The oily and fatty parts, which we call Sulfur, are not extracted from the same part of a compound, nor are they found equally in all things. In some, they are located in the roots and bark; in others, in the flowers, leaves, or fruits; and in some, in the seeds. This Sulfur is easily corrupted <note>Meaning it spoils or decays quickly.</note>, insofar as it dominates the <term>Salt Nitre<note>Potassium nitrate; in this context, it is viewed as a preservative principle that balances the volatile sulfur.</note></term>. This is seen in oils extracted by pressing <note>Cold-pressing seeds or fruits.</note>, which grow thick and putrefy; this does not happen to distilled essences, because they are aided and corrected by the fire.

# CHAPTER IV.

## *On Chemical Operations.*

It is necessary that a craftsman knows how to speak in the terms of his art, and that he knows the tools and utensils which he must use. We will speak first of operations, and particularly of distillations.

Chemists perform three kinds of distillations, which they call: *by ascent* <note>original Latin: *Per aſcenſum*</note>, *by way of the retort* <note>original Latin: *Per medium cornutum*; a retort is a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, often called a "horn" in early chemistry.</note>, and *by descent* <note>original Latin: *Per deſcenſum*</note>. *By ascent* is a distillation <term>distillation: the process of heating a substance to create vapor and then condensing that vapor back into a liquid to purify it</term> which raises the <term>spirits: the volatile or "airy" parts of a substance captured during distillation</term> in the form of smoke, where, finding no way out...
