part, they condense into water, and fall through the pipe of the still-head. **By means of the horned vessel** <note>original: "Per medium cornutum"; this refers to a retort, a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances that require higher heat or are difficult to vaporize</note> is a method or intermediate way for things that cannot be raised easily. **By descent** <note>original: "Per descensum"; a method where the heat is applied from above or the side, forcing the essence to drip downwards into a receiver</note> is for heavy substances. But let us speak of each of these Distillations in particular.

### Distillation by ascent.
<note>original: "per ascensum"; the standard method where vapors rise up, cool, and are collected</note>

**Distillation by ascent** is performed in several ways, according to the substance one wishes to distill. If it is <term>Spirit of wine</term> <note>high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note>, it must be done in a **Bath** <note>a "Bain-marie" or water bath, providing gentle, even heat</note>. If they are aromatic **Essences**, such as Rosemary, Sage, Hyssop, Fennel, Anise, and others of a similar nature, this distillation can only be done in a copper **Alembic** <note>a chemical still consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</note>, especially since a violent fire is required, and a great quantity of herbs to extract a single ounce of Essence; and one finds no glass vessels large enough that also have a <term>cooling pipe</term> <note>original: "tuyau refrigeratoire"; a condenser used to cool vapors back into liquid</note>, even though it is necessary to cool them to prevent the loss of the spirits. ## Sublimation.

MANY have confused **Distillation** with **Sublimation**; it is
very true that in both an eleva-
tion occurs; in one, it is the fluid
and liquid parts [that rise], which is why it is cal-
led distillation, insofar as the va-
pors which rise fall back as water. It
is not the same with **Sublima-
tion**, for it is the most spi-
ritual, light, and dry parts which
rise and adhere to the neck of the ves-
sel. It is to be noted that when one
sublimates mineral or metal-
lic things, the highest elevated parts,
like a fine dust <note>original: "folle farine," literally "wild flour," referring to the finest, most volatile particles that gather at the top of the vessel</note>, are poisonous, and
Medicine absolutely forbids
using them.

### Rectification

**T**HIS Operation is very necessary to make the spirits <note>volatile liquids produced by distillation</note> purer and more refined, and to separate them from any remaining impurities or earthy matter after they have been distilled either by the Retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck</note> or by the Alembic. You shall rectify using a *Bain-Marie* <note>a water bath used for gentle, uniform heating</note> over a gentle or boiling heat, depending on the nature of the substance. The more you rectify, the more you must reduce the heat; for the parts that rise will be increasingly refined.

20      *Chemistry*
        *Calcination.*

**T**here are two kinds of **Calcination** <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical state</note>; one simple, the other corrosive; and both are only for minerals and metals. Simple [calcination] is done by putting your metal or mineral in a **Crucible** <note>a heat-resistant container used for melting substances</note>, and giving it a strong fire until you have reduced the said Metal or Mineral into an impalpable powder. Corrosive [calcination] is done by **strong waters** <note>original: "eaux fortes," a term for concentrated acids, most commonly nitric acid</note>, which reduce the Metals into **calx** <note>original: "chaux," meaning the crumbly oxide or powder left after a metal is treated with heat or acid</note>. The name *Calcination* is very poorly applied to this operation, since Calcination is destruction; and gold and silver are not destroyed by strong waters, but only corroded and altered; since they recover their body <note>return to their solid, metallic form</note> in a violent fire, this is not Calcination, but only an **imperfect dissolution**.

        *Cohobation.*

**T**O **COHOBATE** <note>a redistillation process</note> is nothing other than putting what has been distilled back onto what remains at the bottom of the distilling vessel, so that the **spiritual part** <note>the volatile essence or refined vapor of the substance</note> penetrates the mass more easily, and so that it...

For that which is done in the Retort <term>Retort: A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling and condensing substances</term>, the fire must be gentle, for fear of giving the distilled substances a bad odor.

### Coagulation.

**COAGULATING** is to make a liquid thing into a firm consistency: it is done in two ways, one by fire, the other by cold: that of the fire is when you have extracted some tincture; you will evaporate the moisture in an earthenware pan or glass vessel until it reaches the consistency of Honey or an Extract, according to the use for which you wish to employ it. That which is done by cold is for dissolved Salts; when you have evaporated <note>original: "evaporer." The text uses the tilde over the 'o' in "selõ" as a shorthand for "selon" (according to).</note> two-thirds of the water, and a small film <note>A "pellicule" or skin forming on the surface, indicating the solution is saturated.</note> forms on the surface, you will place your vessel in the cold of the cellar, and a portion will coagulate into Crystals, which you will finish drying in a Crucible <term>Crucible: A ceramic or metal container used to melt substances or dry them at very high temperatures</term>, and keep in a well-sealed vessel: evaporate as above, and place in the cold until no more crystals are formed.

22 Chemistry

*Filtration.*

TO FILTER, in common terms, is to pass or strain a substance to make it clearer and cleaner. It is performed using gray paper, a Hippocras bag <note>a conical fabric filter, usually made of wool or linen, named after the Greek physician Hippocrates</note>, or by a small tongue <note>a strip of cloth or wick used for capillary filtration, where liquid is drawn up and over the edge of a vessel</note>, as many times as the operator deems necessary. This operation is essential for the extraction of tinctures and salts.

*Desiccation.*

CALCINATION and Desiccation <note>drying</note> are almost the same thing, differing only by degree. Desiccation is intended to dry a substance and make it capable of absorbing the liquid that will be added to it, or to become impregnated with said liquid so that it becomes easier to grind and pound.

*Dulcoration.*

TO DULCORATE <note>literally "to sweeten"; in this context, it means to wash away salts or acids</note> is to wash the calx <note>original: "Chaux"; the powdery metallic oxide remaining after heating or acid treatment</note> of metals to remove the corrosiveness that the aqua fortis <note>original: "eaux fortes"; literally "strong waters," referring to nitric acid</note> might have imparted to them. It also serves to dulcorate the sulfur of metals and min-

## Inclination.

**INCLINATION** occurs when the substance has been washed and purified, and has settled at the bottom of the vessel; one pours off the water from the top by inclination <note>original: "par inclination" — this refers to what we now call decanting, or carefully tilting the vessel to pour out liquid without disturbing the sediment</note>, which separates from the material easily.

## Amalgamation.

**WITHOUT** seeking the origin of this word, which according to some Authors is only for the mixing of quicksilver <note>original: "argent vif," literally "living silver" or mercury</note> with the Calx of Metals; I say that to amalgamate is to mix, incorporate, and grind a mass with a liquid in such a way that they cannot be separated.

## Digestion.

**DIGESTION** explains itself. It is nothing other than a gentle and penetrating heat and diges- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "digestive."</note>

Menstruum.

MENSTRUUM is a liquid which serves to aid in drawing out and extracting all kinds of spirits, essences, tinctures, and salts; to digest and decompose all things that require moisture. There are several kinds; depending on the nature of the substance that one wishes to extract or decompose, one uses spirit of wine <note>original: "esprit de vin"; high-proof alcohol or brandy.</note>, spirit of dew <note>Dew was highly prized by alchemists as it was thought to contain "celestial balm" or pure ethereal energy.</note>, <term>aqua fortis|Literally 'strong water'; a historical name for nitric acid.</term>, lemon juices, distilled vinegar, common water, or distilled water, according as the Artist <note>The practitioner of the chemical or alchemical arts.</note> deems appropriate.

Fermentation.

TAKE the things you wish to ferment, and crush them if they are fresh; and if they are dry, they must be moistened and placed in a glass vessel which you will cork, and place in the cellar in sand; you will leave it there until they begin to turn sour, for they will then be sufficiently fermented to distill them.

### Deliquescence

I have spoken previously of <term>Deliquescence</term><note>original: "Defuillance"; in early chemistry, this refers to the process where a salt or calcined powder absorbs enough moisture from the air to dissolve into a liquid.</note> in the article on the Vapor Bath; it is for substances that have been <term>calcined</term><note>heated to a high temperature to reduce them to a powder</note> and moistened, which dissolve in the cold; these fall by deliquescence, and for those which are expressed under the press.

### Reverberation

**Reverberation** is like **calcination**, except that **reverberation** is performed in a closed vessel, and **calcination** is performed over an open fire. One makes use of this operation for two purposes; one, so that the spirits may be calcined together with the bodies; and the other, so that the substance one wishes to calcine may have more strength and be more perfect.

### Precipitation

**PRECIPITATION** is an operation which one uses after having performed the <term>dissolution</term><note>the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid solvent</note> of some metal by <term>aqua fortis</term><note>original: "eau forte"; literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid, used to dissolve metals.</note>, and it is reduced to <term>calx</term><note>original: "chaux"; the powder or "lime" that remains after a metal has been dissolved in acid or burnt.</note>. One uses salt water to—

->**Saltwater (Brine)**<-

**SALTWATER** <note>original: "L’EAV marinée"</note> is nothing other than common water in which one has dissolved as much common salt as it could possibly take. It is used to precipitate the **calx** <note>the powdery substance, now known as an oxide, left after a metal has been heated or chemically treated</note> of metals, as has been mentioned above.

->**Stratification**<-

**TO STRATIFY** is to place one substance in the form of a **bed** <note>a layer</note> in a vessel, and place another substance on top, and begin again bed upon bed until all your materials are placed. It is performed in two different vessels, namely the **crucible** <note>a heat-resistant container used for melting minerals or metals</note> and the **cucurbit** <note>a gourd-shaped glass vessel used as the lower part of a distillation apparatus</note>. The crucible is for mineral substances; the cucurbit for those that one wishes to **distill** or put into **digestion** <note>the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to soften or extract its essence</note>. Chemists call this operation **layer upon layer** <note>original: "Stratum super stratum"</note>.

<meta>This page appears to be from a seventeenth-century pharmaceutical manual, likely "Le Médecin charitable" (The Charitable Physician) by Philibert Guybert, a popular text designed to teach people how to prepare their own medicines at home. ->Charitable & Easy. 29<-
### Torrification ###

WHEN it is necessary to roast <note>original: "torrifier"; the process of parching or drying a substance over heat to alter its properties</note> a compound <term>mixte: a chemical or medicinal mixture of various substances</term>, it is because it contains impurities that one wants to correct by dissipating the superfluous or dangerous moisture in which it abounds. It is done like this: Reduce the materials you wish to roast—or rather, to purify and dry—into a powder if they can be crushed, or cut them into slices. Put them into a suitable vessel, that is to say, one made of tin, because it cannot withstand violent heat, and a very gentle heat is required here; place it on a stove <term>rechaud: a portable heater or brazier</term> and stir constantly until your materials no longer give off smoke. Be careful of this smoke, for it is dangerous. For Opium, Hellebore, Scammony <note>a plant resin used as a strong purgative</note>, Antimony, and several others, they are prepared in different ways. For some, the flame of sulfur is used for roasting, by putting the drugs into small squares of paper which are passed over it until they no longer smoke; some carry them
->B iij<-

*Decrepitation.*

THIS operation has only one object, which is Salt, and is only practiced to purify it. In all kinds of Salt, there are <term>fiery spirits<note>In early modern chemistry, "spirits" (esprits) referred to volatile substances or gases trapped within a solid that were released during heating.</note></term>, which cannot be subdued; they flare up and make a very great noise when they are enclosed, and break everything that opposes their violence; but Chemistry avoids all these accidents by purifying the Salts of their wicked qualities. This operation is performed in an earthen vessel that resists fire, in which you will put the Salt you wish to <term>decrepitate<note>Decrepitation is the crackling sound produced when heating certain salts, caused by the sudden expansion of water trapped within the crystals.</note></term>, and place the pot, covered with something so heavy that it can resist [the pressure], over glowing coals; a great noise will occur: when it has subsided, let your Salt cool, and it will be decrepitated and prepared.

### Dead Head
<note>original: "Teſte morte", often known by the Latin term *caput mortuum*.</note>

The **dead head** is a substance that is fit for nothing; it is insipid, without taste and without flavor, from which nothing more can be extracted. This is why it is called "dead head," or "damned" or "condemned" earth. <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, this refers to the useless solid residue remaining in a flask after all volatile components have been driven off by heat.</note>

### CHAPTER V.

#### Of the vessels.

THE vessels suitable for distillations **by ascent** <note>original: "per aſcenſum"; a process where vapors rise upward to condense</note> are the copper **Alembic** <term>Alembic: A classic distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling pot and a cooling cap.</term> with its cap and cooling pipe; the Recipient <note>The vessel that collects the final product</note> is called a **Matrass**. <term>Matrass: A round-bottomed glass flask with a long neck, used for boiling or collecting liquids.</term> To distill in a **Water-bath** <note>original: "Bain-Marie"; a container of hot water used to provide gentle, even heat</note>, and in fires of ash, sand, filings, and manure, one needs a glass gourd or **cucurbit** <term>Cucurbit: The lower part of a still, named for its gourd-like shape, which holds the liquid to be distilled.</term> with its beaked cap, and its Recipient, which is a long-necked Matrass. To distill **by the horned vessel** <note>original: "per medium cornutum"; this refers to using a retort, a flask with a long, downward-pointing neck that looks like a horn.</note>...

32               **Chemistry**

Two retorts are needed, or else a **retort** and a large receiver called a **balloon** <note>original: "Balon"; a large, round-bottomed glass flask used to collect the products of distillation.</note>.

To **sublime** <note>A process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid form to purify it.</note>, one needs a **matrass** <term>matrass: a long-necked glass flask, also known as a bolt-head</term> or **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit: the lower, gourd-shaped part of a still that holds the liquid to be distilled</term> with its head pierced at the top, or an **aludel** <note>A pear-shaped earthenware pot used as a condenser in the sublimation process.</note>.

To **circulate** <note>A method of continuous distillation where the liquid is evaporated and then flows back into the same vessel to be re-distilled.</note>, one needs two matrasses, or a **pelican** <note>A specialized circulatory vessel with two curved side-arms that resemble the neck of a pelican feeding its young.</note>; in its absence, a small gourd with a **blind alembic** <note>original: "Alembic aveugle"; a still-head without a spout or beak, used to force vapors to condense and fall back into the vessel.</note>.

For **digestion** <note>The process of softening or decomposing a substance using gentle, consistent heat.</note> and **putrefaction**, gourds with their blind heads are required; and when the putrefaction or digestion is complete, if one wishes to distill, one only has to change the blind head and put on one with a beak along with its receiver.

To **calcine** <note>To heat a substance to high temperatures in order to oxidize it or reduce it to a powder.</note>, one needs a **crucible** or a pot that can withstand the fire.

To **reverberate** <note>A heating technique where the flame or heat is reflected (reverberated) off the roof of a furnace onto the material.</note>, one needs two crucibles, or two pots well **luted** <note>original: "lutez"; sealed together using a "lute," a paste made of clay and other materials to make the joint airtight.</note> one on top of the other.

To **decrepitate** <note>To heat salts until they make a crackling sound as their crystalline structure breaks down.</note>, one needs a covered pot.

To **torrefy** <note>To roast or parch a substance over a fire to remove moisture.</note>, a tin vessel.

It is also necessary to have earthenware pans, jugs, and vials. The quantity of vessels does not make the skil— <note>The text ends mid-word: "l’ha-" likely refers to "l'habileté" or "the skill/dexterity of the artist."</note>

34          **Chemistry**

...and lute <term>lute: a clay or cement paste used to seal chemical apparatus and protect it from heat</term> your retorts <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term> and flasks, or other vessels, which you will let dry gently in the air without fire or sun. This lute resists the fire.

To repair broken vessels, you shall reduce quicklime <term>quicklime: a caustic, powdery substance (calcium oxide) obtained by heating limestone</term> into powder, and mix it with egg white; you shall soak a very fine linen cloth in it, and apply it promptly over the cracks.

To lute the receivers <term>receiver: a vessel that collects the liquid produced during distillation</term> and the cucurbits <term>cucurbit: the lower part of a distilling apparatus, named for its gourd-like shape</term> with their heads <note>the 'head' refers to the alembic cap placed on top of the cucurbit to direct vapors</note>, you need only starch-paste and paper.

Having taught how to lute the vessels, it is only right to provide the manner of trimming and piercing them.

To pierce them, melt some sulfur in a crucible, soak a string in it, and wrap it around the area where you wish to pierce the vessel; set it on fire, and when the string is almost burned, throw a little water upon it; the piece of glass will fall out. <note>This technique uses "thermal shock" to create a clean break in the glass.</note>

To break the neck of the vessels, expose them to the flame of a candle, turn them until they are very hot, and dip them in water...

# CHAPTER VII.

## Concerning Fires.

THERE are several kinds of fires; there are those that are natural and those that are artificial. The first is:

**The Sun’s fire**, to which one exposes things that are easy to dissolve or resolve <note>To "resolve" a substance was to break it down into its constituent elements or "principles."</note>. In using this fire, one must observe that the vessels are never full, because they will break.

**Pigeon dung fire**, which is used for digestions and putrefactions <note>Digestion is the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat; putrefaction is the intentional decomposition of organic matter.</note>, must be stimulated by horse manure.

**Horse manure fire** serves the same purposes: it must be renewed every three days. <note>Decaying manure produces a steady, low heat, acting as a natural incubator for chemical reactions.</note>

**Lamp fire** is a constant, steady fire.

**Charcoal fire**, for distillations using a <term>retort</term> <note>original: "cornuë"; a glass flask with a long, downward-pointing neck used to distill substances that require high heat.</note>, and **wood fire** for the cooling <term>alembic</term> <note>A specialized apparatus for distillation, consisting of a boiling pot and a cooling cap (the head) to collect vapor.</note>.

It is absolutely necessary to kno— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("sçavoir"), likely leading into "know the degrees of heat."</note>

## CHAPTER IX.

### *On Chemical Symbols.*

The Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists and early practitioners of "Natural Philosophy," the precursor to modern science.</note> did everything in their power to avoid making their operations common knowledge. They hid the names of materials, operations, and vessels <term>vessels: laboratory equipment like flasks, retorts, and crucibles</term> under certain characters <note>original: "caracteres" — here meaning symbolic glyphs or shorthand signs used to keep recipes secret.</note>, which has resulted in many secrets remaining unused. This is why I have chosen to explain them for the benefit of those who will read this book, to facilitate all kinds of operations for them, and to spare them from having to look elsewhere for their explanations.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a chapter dedicated to decoding alchemical symbols, which the author argues were used by earlier 'philosophers' to keep their discoveries secret from the public.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, chemical symbols, laboratory vessels, secrecy, natural philosophy, history of chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as a reference guide for apothecary weights and measurements, likely concluding a section of a medical or pharmaceutical manual. The previous context is unknown, but the text transitions from medical instruction to the practical standardizations of the era.</meta>

->Charitable & Easy. 43<-

Since observing weights is necessary in Medicine, I wanted to insert them at the end of this Part. Here is their explanation.

The **grain** <note>The grain was the smallest unit of the apothecary system, traditionally based on the weight of a single dry grain of barley.</note> is worth the weight of a large grain of barley.
The **half Scruple** is worth ten grains.
The **Scruple** is twenty grains.
The **half Dram** is 30 grains.
The **Dram** <note>A unit of weight in the apothecaries' system; one dram is 1/8th of an ounce.</note> is 60 grains.
The **half Ounce** is 4 Drams.
The **Ounce** is eight Drams.
The **medical pound** <note>The "medical" or apothecary pound (12 ounces) was smaller than the standard commercial pound (16 ounces).</note> is twelve ounces.

<term>Ana</term> <note>original: "Ana"; a term derived from the Greek *ana*, meaning "of each."</note> signifies equal parts of several different drugs that go into the composition of a remedy, which are written before this word **Ana**.
<term>Handful</term> <note>original: "Manipule"; from the Latin *manipulus*.</note> signifies what can be contained in the hand.
<term>Pinch</term> <note>original: "Pugille"; from the Latin *pugillus*.</note> is what one can hold with three fingers, or a pinch.

<summary>This page defines the standard units of weight and measurement used in 17th-century pharmacy, ranging from single grains of barley to the twelve-ounce medical pound.           *Concerning the Spirit of Tartar.*

Wine produces a Tartar, or Salt <note>Specifically potassium bitartrate, which naturally crystallizes in wine barrels.</note>, which attaches to the walls of the vessels in which wine is kept. The Tartar of white wine is the best. Those who have treated the virtues and perfections of the vine say that white wine is the male, and the red is the female. ### Cream or Crystal of Tartar

**TAKE** Tartar <note>Potassium bitartrate, a sediment found in wine barrels</note>, as has been described, and grind it into a fine powder, over which you will pour water into a glass vessel to a height of six fingers. Boil the said water for half an hour, and pour it by inclination <note>original: "par inclination" — the act of decanting or pouring off liquid to leave the sediment behind</note> into another glass vessel; put more water over the Tartar remaining at the bottom of the vessel, boil, and pour off again as previously described. Repeat until all your Tartar is dissolved; take all these waters and boil them until they are reduced by three-quarters. You will place the remaining portion in a cold place; in twelve hours' time, crystals will form on the surface, which you will lift off with a silver spoon and dry—

You may <term>rectify</term> <note>To purify a liquid through repeated distillation.</note> it as many times as you please, and <term>dephlegm</term> <note>The process of removing the "phlegm" or watery, tasteless part of a substance to concentrate its active components.</note> it each time. It should be noted that all acidic things are cold <note>In the medical theory of the time, substances were classified by "qualities" (hot, cold, moist, or dry). Acids were considered "cold."</note> and that they possess an insipid <term>phlegm</term> that emerges first. One can also extract an oil and a salt from vinegar, which I omit here as I do not know their medicinal virtues.

Distilled vinegar is used to extract tinctures; it dissolves pearls and corals. White and distilled vinegar is used to cleanse greasy and oily complexions.

->**Of the leaves, seeds, and ashes**<-

When placed as a <term>poultice</term> <note>A soft, moist mass of material, typically of plant material or flour, applied to the body to relieve soreness and inflammation.</note> on the stomach, they cool it; the distilled water of the said leaves and tendrils stops dysentery and the spitting of blood.

<meta>This page appears to be from a 17th-century treatise on "charitable chemistry," likely by Marie Meurdrac. It continues a discussion on the medicinal virtues of distilled essences, specifically focusing on rosemary.</meta>

58 ->*Chemistry*<-

Rupescissa <note>Johannes de Rupescissa (c. 1310–1366) was a Franciscan friar and alchemist famous for his theories on the "Quintessence" or the medicinal spirit of wine and plants.</note> places it among the rank of tempered things: Avicenna, Mattioli, Dioscorides, Daléchamps <note>A "who's who" of ancient and Renaissance medicine: Avicenna (Persian polymath), Pietro Andrea Mattioli (Italian physician), Dioscorides (ancient Greek botanist), and Jacques Daléchamps (French doctor).</note> and others attribute to it powers and faculties capable of regenerating man and giving him new strength. Most of these authors were ignorant of the preparations of <term>simples|medicinal plants used on their own rather than as part of a complex compound</term>, and prescribed them completely raw or in coarse decoctions; but if they say such wonders of it when it is poorly prepared, what can we write and guarantee when it is well-prepared, purified, and separated from its bad qualities?

A modern philosopher was right to say that every man who loved life should have rosemary essence in his house as a universal antidote to all kinds of ills. I have used it <note>The author uses the feminine form "servie" here, confirming the female voice of the chemist—a rarity for this time period.</note> successfully and have performed wonderful cures with it. One can take from six to ten drops of its essence in wine, or in a spoonful of sugared water in the morning on an empty stomach. It protects against all infected air <note>Refers to the "miasma theory," the belief that diseases like the plague were spread by foul-smelling air.</note>, cures jaundice, cuts through <term>gross humors|thick, sluggish bodily fluids that were thought to cause illness in Galenic medicine</term>, gladdens the heart, and drives away melancholy.

<summary>The author advocates for the superior medicinal power of distilled rosemary essence over raw herbs, citing classical authorities while providing practical dosages for daily health and emotional well-being.</summary>

->Charitable & Easy. 61<-

### On the Essence of Sage.

*IT* is a specific remedy for all diseases of the brain; it stops and diverts all kinds of discharges <note>original: "fluxions"; in early modern medicine, these were "flowings" of harmful humors or fluids to specific parts of the body</note>; it expels a dead fetus <note>original: "fruit mort"; sage was historically believed to stimulate the uterus to aid in difficult labors or miscarriages</note> and helps nature to push it out; the dose is six drops up to ten in a spoonful of brandy <note>original: "eau de vie," literally "water of life"</note> or sugar water; it enables a sterile woman to conceive by taking it for eight mornings in a row, as has been said, and by abstaining during this time from the company of her husband.

### On Sage Water.

*THIS* water cleanses <note>original: "mondifie," a technical term used in surgery for cleaning and purifying a wound to promote healing</note> wounds if they are washed with it; it darkens the hair when used as a rinse, strengthens the brain and the limbs, stops nosebleeds <note>original: "sang tirée par le nez," literally blood drawn through the nose</note>, heals stings, relieves toothaches, and firms the gums, by washing all the aforementioned with the said water while it is slightly warm.

<summary>This page outlines the medicinal virtues of sage essence and sage water, highlighting their roles in neurological health, reproductive aid, and topical wound care.</summary>
<keywords>sage, herbal essence, fertility, midwifery, brain health, dentistry, wound care, brandy</keywords>

62 ->*Chemistry*<-

### *Of the Tincture or Extract of Sage.*

THIS Tincture is good against cold pains <note>In the humoral theory of the time, "cold" pains were thought to be caused by an excess of phlegm or a lack of metabolic heat.</note> when cast upon glowing coals. The smoke, taken from below <note>This refers to medicinal fumigation, where a patient would sit over the rising smoke to treat internal conditions.</note>, stops the loss of blood in women. The weight of an *écu* <term>écu: a French crown coin used here as a weight measurement, approximately 3 to 4 grams.</term> taken in pills stops vomiting. The same dose diluted in half a glass of white wine, drunk for three mornings while fasting, cures quartan fever <note>A form of malaria where the fever returns every fourth day.</note>.

### *The Salt of Sage.*

THIS Salt ought to be called "Blessed" for the benefits it produces; it is universal for all kinds of diseases when taken in a vehicle <term>vehicle: a base liquid—such as water, wine, or broth—used to help the body absorb the medicine.</term> suited to the ailment. The dose is from one scruple <term>scruple: an apothecary unit of weight equivalent to about 1.3 grams.</term> up to two: it has the same virtues as the essence, the water, and the tincture; reunite all four parts together, and add a little deer marrow, put it all in a glass vessel of which a third shall be left empty, and seal it well; expose it for one month to the Sun, and you will have a Balm for all kinds of wounds and pains.

Charitable & Easy. 63

# On Hyssop

THIS plant <note>Hyssop (*Hyssopus officinalis*) is a fragrant herb in the mint family, used since antiquity for its supposed cleansing and medicinal properties</note> is one of the most necessary in medicine, particularly for women's ailments; nonetheless, one finds little of it prepared as it should be.

## On the Essence

The Essence is sovereign for lung diseases arising from cold causes <note>In the "humoral" medical theory of the time, diseases were classified by temperature and moisture; "cold" ailments of the lungs often referred to conditions involving excess phlegm or congestion</note>; it cures the <term>pale colors</term> <note>original: "palles couleurs," a historical term for chlorosis or anemia, often attributed to young women in early modern medicine</note> of girls, and brings on their <term>purgations</term> <note>menstruation</note>; it helps expel the <term>afterbirth</term> <note>the placenta</note> which remains after childbirth; it aids breathing and gives strength; the dose is eight to ten drops taken in its own water, or white wine.

## On Hyssop Water

THIS water serves as a vehicle <note>In pharmacy, a "vehicle" is an inactive substance used to carry or deliver the active medicinal ingredient</note> for its Essence; it is suitable for all the aforementioned diseases. The dose is from half a glass to a full glass.

<summary>This page describes the medicinal virtues of Hyssop, focusing on its "Essence" and "Water" for treating respiratory issues and various gynecological conditions like anemia and post-partum recovery.</summary>
<keywords>Hyssop, women's health, respiratory medicine, essence, chlorosis, menstruation, humoral medicine, botanical remedies</keywords>

64                *Chemistry*

*On the Tincture or extract of Hyssop.*

Pills are made from this Tincture, sharpened with ten or twelve drops of its Essence. One <term>Drachm</term> <note>A unit of weight in the apothecaries' system, approximately 3.9 grams.</note> of <term>salt of Mars</term> <note>A chemical term for iron salts, often iron sulfate, believed to have "strengthening" properties.</note>, half a Drachm of <term>epithyme</term> <note>Dodder of Thyme (*Cuscuta epithymum*), a parasitic plant used in early modern medicine to purge humors.</note>, one drop of Essence of clove, two of cinnamon, four grains of tincture of Saffron, the whole incorporated into three Drachms of the tincture, from which four doses shall be made. These bring back <term>purgations</term> <note>Here referring to the menstrual cycle; these ingredients were commonly used to treat amenorrhea (stopped periods).</note> that have long been halted; by taking them in the morning, one must continue until recovery.

*On the Salt of Hyssop.*

It has the same virtues as its Essence, water, and tincture.

*On Tansy.*

<term>Tansy</term> <note>A bitter, aromatic herb (*Tanacetum vulgare*) traditionally used to treat intestinal worms and digestive ailments.</note> is almost unknown in France, although it has very particular qualities. The Germans and the <term>Grisons</term> <note>Inhabitants of Graubünden, the easternmost region of Switzerland.</note> use it like <term>Theriac</term> <note>A famous ancient "universal" medicine or antidote made of dozens of ingredients; the author suggests Tansy is powerful enough to be used as a stand-in.</note> against all sorts of diseases; I have tested it for all the following diseases.

## Of the Essence of <term>Tansy</term> <note>Tansy (*Tanacetum vulgare*) is a bitter herb historically used in folk medicine, though its oil is now known to be potentially toxic due to high thujone content.</note>.

**IT** facilitates women’s childbirth when ten to twelve drops are taken in two spoonfuls of <term>cinnamon water</term> <note>A common "vehicle" or base for medicine, made by distilling water with cinnamon to provide a warming effect and better flavor.</note>; four drops taken in two spoonfuls of its own water kills and expels worms. The same dose taken in <term>parsley water</term> <note>Parsley was a standard treatment for urinary issues in early modern medicine.</note> makes one urinate easily, and dissolves and breaks the stone <note>Refers to kidney or bladder stones, a common ailment in the period.</note>.

## Of Tansy Water.

**THIS** water is not at all unpleasant when <term>Narbonne honey</term> <note>A highly sought-after honey from the Languedoc region of France, traditionally harvested from bees feeding on rosemary.</note> has been added to it; it expels worms from small children when they are given four or five spoonfuls to drink in the morning for three or four consecutive days during the <term>waning of the moon</term> <note>Medical treatments intended to "decrease" an ailment or purge the body were traditionally timed with the waning moon, following the principles of astrological medicine.</note>. In the absence of the Essence, one can give a glass of this water to a woman in labor; it will facilitate her delivery; and if one takes a glass of this water for eight mornings in a row before giving birth, and walks for an hour in the room, she will give birth happily and easily.

**Water against scars, & marks

TAKE roots of wild Cucumbers, and of Iris <note>original: "Flambe," referring to the Florentine Iris or Yellow Flag, whose roots were frequently used in early modern skin preparations</note>, of each a half pound; roots of Marshmallows, and bulbs of white Lilies, of each one pound; ripe Grapes half a pound, leaves of Beans, and of Pellitory-of-the-wall, of each one handful; flowers of Water-lily & Mallows, of each two handfuls; barley bread crumbs one pound: infuse the whole in a pint of white wine, and a pint of Goat's milk: you shall add to the infusion a Radish cut into rounds, of the <term>four cold seeds</term> <note>In traditional medicine, these are the seeds of cucumber, melon, gourd, and watermelon, believed to have "cooling" effects on the body’s humors</note>, of each a half ounce; urine of a young girl aged nine to ten years <note>Urine from prepubescent children was a common ingredient in historical pharmacy, valued for its perceived purity and ammonia content which acted as a cleanser</note>, half a pound: distill the whole in a boiling bath <note>A distillation method similar to a "Bain-marie," using the heat of boiling water to gently evaporate the mixture</note>. This water is excellent against all sorts of spots that appear on the face; it removes scars, and erases the marks of smallpox, and of burns.

270          Chemistry

*Water that whitens the face.*

YOU shall take the shells of twelve fresh eggs, one pound of vine tears water <note>original: "eau de pleurs de vigne"; the sap that exudes from grapevines when they are pruned in the spring, historically used in cosmetics</note>, one ounce of common salt, and half a pound of spring water: distil the whole thing through a <term>Retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> in a <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "feu de sable"; a method of heating where the vessel is placed in a container of sand to ensure even, indirect heat</note>. This water corrodes the skin and renders the complexion white and delicate. <note>The "corrosive" nature mentioned here suggests a chemical peel effect, likely from the high mineral and acid content resulting from the distillation.</note> It must be applied for eight days, morning and evening; afterwards, it should only be applied once a week.

*Water against freckles and redness of the face.*

TAKE white vinegar, rose-water, and lemon juice, one pound <note>original: "li-mon"; likely a printing error for "livre" (pound), as "limon" (lemon) was just mentioned as an ingredient</note> of each, and four ounces of <term>native sulfur</term> <note>original: "Soufre vif"; naturally occurring sulfur, also known as brimstone</note> in powder: distil the whole through a Retort over a <term>wheel fire</term> <note>original: "feu de rouë"; a technique where the distillation vessel is surrounded by a circle of coals to provide intense, even heat</note>. One must often wash freckles and redness with this water, and place a cloth <note>original: "liuge"; a typo for "linge" (linen or cloth)</note> soaked in it upon the face during the night.

# Charitable & Easy. 271

## Water for skin eruptions on the face.

YOU will take rose water, nightshade <note>original: "Morelle"; likely Black Nightshade (*Solanum nigrum*), which was frequently used in topical cooling remedies.</note> water, plantain water, and white vinegar, half a pound of each: dissolve in these prepared common salt and sal ammoniac <term>Sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, a volatile salt used in early chemistry and medicine.</term>, half an ounce of each; then distill using a retort <term>Retort: a glassware vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</term> over a sand bath, and cohobate <term>Cohobate: the alchemical practice of pouring the distilled liquid back onto the solid residue remaining in the vessel and re-distilling it to concentrate the "spirits."</term> twice: at the end, use a strong fire to make the spirits of the salts and the vinegar rise. This water must be applied with a feather to the skin eruptions <note>The original "dartres" refers to tetters—a general term for scaly skin conditions like eczema, psoriasis, or herpes.</note>, and applied three or four times a day.

## Water for freckles on the face.

TAKE three ounces of lemon juice, four ounces of white vinegar, one pound of powdered alum, and half a pound of ox gall <term>Ox gall: the bile of an ox, historically used as a detergent and in cosmetic recipes to clear the complexion.</term>: distill it all in a boiling water bath, and apply the water that distills with a feather onto the freckles <note>The original "roussures" refers to freckles or reddish spots on the skin.</note>.

## Concerning the oil and water of Talc.

### Oil of Talc.

**MANY** assert that they know how to
extract the oil of <term>Talc|In this period, "Talc" usually referred to Muscovite mica. It was the most prized cosmetic ingredient of the 17th century, rumored to provide a permanent, translucent whiteness to the skin.</term> without

common, nor so easy as they
claim: I believe that few people
know this secret, and that it is very rare.
Here are some methods of preparing
Talc, which are good for the complexion,
and are easy to do, and I can assure
[you] that their effect is very good.

remove the shells, and cut them
in half, remove the yolks, and put
in their place some pulverized <note>original: "Talc de Venise." Venetian Talc was considered the purest variety and was a staple in high-end Renaissance and Baroque beauty recipes.</note> Venice Talc—

*Ointment to keep the complexion fresh and smooth.*

You shall take May butter <note>Butter made from the milk of cows grazing on spring grass, prized in early modern medicine for its supposed purity and healing properties</note> and bull tallow, half a pound of each: melt them together in a new pot with half a septier <note>An old French liquid measure; a half-septier is approximately 240 milliliters or about one cup</note> of rosewater; then strain them through a cloth into another pot containing half a septier of rosewater. When the mixture is cold, you shall lift it out with a spoon and place it in a marble mortar, and incorporate it with six ounces of ceruse <term>White lead; a common but highly toxic lead-based pigment used in 17th-century cosmetics to whiten the skin</term> that has been well washed several times in rosewater, dried in the sun, and reduced to an impalpable powder. Then keep it in a very clean pot, and place sugar on top of it, as has been described <note>Sugar was often used as a preservative or to seal the surface of ointments</note>. You shall apply this ointment morning and evening, and wipe it off an hour after having applied it.

->Charitable & easy. 283<-

### Ointment to nourish the complexion, & against the marks of smallpox.

**TAKE** two pounds of very fatty lard from the throat of a male pig, scrape it with a wooden knife, and remove all the small fibers and membranes: take half a pound of pig fat, from which you will remove all the small skins, and cut it into pieces. Put them to soak for nine days in river water, which must be changed twice a day, and knead them well with your hands when the water is changed; then melt them in an earthenware pot with Rose water, and Plantain <note>Plantain water was a common herbal distillate made from *Plantago major*, used historically to soothe skin inflammations and "heat."</note> water, half a pound of each, two spoonfuls of clove water, one Lemon, and one Orange cut into slices; six Capendu <note>A traditional French variety of apple, also known as "Court-pendu," highly valued in the early modern period for its fragrance and medicinal properties.</note> apples, peeled and crushed in a marble mortar, from which the cores have been removed; two ounces of Florentine Iris <term>Orris root: the dried and aged rhizome of the *Iris florentina*, which smells of violets and was a staple in historical cosmetics</term> cut into slices: cover the pot, and set it to infuse for twenty-four hours in a Water Bath <note>Original: "au Bain" - referring to a Bain-marie, a method of indirect heating that prevents delicate ingredients from burning.</note>.

<summary>This page details a complex recipe for a medicinal pomade designed to restore the skin's texture and fade scars left by smallpox. The process involves a multi-day purification of animal fats followed by an infusion of aromatic fruits and botanical waters.</summary>

*Another excellent ointment.*

TAKE one ounce of fine, very white pearls, and crush them in a mortar, and put them into a glass vessel; pour distilled white vinegar over them until it floats two fingers <note>A "finger" was a common informal unit of measurement, approximately 3/4 of an inch or 2 centimeters.</note> deep above them: place the vessel on warm ashes to aid the dissolution of the said pearls. When they are dissolved, you shall pour off the vinegar by decanting <note>The original French "par inclination" refers to the process of tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the sediment behind.</note>, and wash the dissolved matter two or three times with orange flower or rose water; then you shall incorporate it with two parts of the sheep's foot ointment mentioned above. In the absence of the pearl solution, one may mix with the said ointment some Venice Talc <term>Venice Talc: a high-quality variety of mica or soapstone used in early modern cosmetics to provide a shimmering, white complexion</term> reduced to an impalpable powder. This ointment is applied morning and evening, and is very excellent.

*Ointment against black, white, red, and greenish spots*
*that appear on the face.*

TAKE lemon juice and egg whites in equal parts; beat them together very well, place them in an earthen pot <note>The original text uses "tertine," a likely variant of "terrine," a glazed earthenware vessel used for cooking or apothecary work.</note> over the fire, with a little chicken fat, and stir constantly until the whole reaches the consistency of an ointment. It must be applied every evening, and continued until the aforementioned spots have disappeared.

### Another red for the face.

**You** shall take one ounce of powdered Brazilwood <note>original: "bois de Bresil"; a tropical wood used extensively since the Middle Ages to produce red dye.</note>, and set it to infuse for twenty-four hours over warm ashes in strong distilled vinegar, so that the vinegar stands two fingers <note>A common historical measurement referring to the width of two fingers.</note> above the said powder. After this, you will add two pounds of water to it, and boil the whole until it is reduced by three parts <note>That is, boiled down until only one-quarter of the original liquid remains.</note>. When this is done, you will add half a quarteron <term>an archaic measurement, roughly equivalent to a quarter of a pound</term> of powdered Alum, and half an ounce of isinglass <term>original: "colle de poisson"; a high-quality collagen/gelatin obtained from the swim bladders of fish, used here as a binder.</term> cut into pieces. When it has dissolved, strain the mixture and put it into boxes. It is applied as described above.

### Another red.

**TAKE** one ounce of well-pounded red Sandalwood, and pour over it one pound of distilled vinegar. Boil it until it is reduced by three parts, then add a pinch of powdered Alum and two spoonfuls of Rose-water, in which some gum Tragacanth <term>original: "gomme Adragant"; a natural gum obtained from the dried sap of legumes, used to thicken the cosmetic.</term> has been dissolved. Strain the mixture and put it in a box.

### CHAPTER VIII.

#### Ox Gall.

->How it is prepared in Montpellier.<-

TAKE four or five ox galls and empty them into a large earthenware bowl; add one ounce each of alum and <term>glass salt</term><note>original: "sel de verre"; a saline scum or byproduct gathered from the surface of molten glass, used in early chemistry and pharmacy.</note> reduced to a powder. Whip the whole mixture with a handful of rods for the space of two or three hours, until everything is like foam; then you will filter it through a piece of cloth and allow it to pass through at its own pace. Take the liquid that has passed through and place it in a double glass vial with two ounces of Borax, two <term>drams</term><note>original: "gros"; a historical unit of weight equivalent to roughly 3.8 grams.</note> of Camphor, and one dram of <term>Sublimate</term><note>Likely corrosive sublimate (mercuric chloride). While highly toxic, it was frequently used in small amounts in historical skin lighteners and medicinal washes.</note>. Expose the vial to the sun for fifteen days, shaking it three or four times each day; then you will place the said vial in a pit in the cellar, in such a way that it is entirely covered with earth, and leave it there for forty days; then

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for a chemical preparation of ox gall, involving whipping, filtering, and a long period of solar exposure and subterranean aging. Such preparations were typically used in early modern cosmetics to clear the complexion or in artistic processes like paper marbling.</summary>
<keywords>ox gall, Montpellier, chemistry, pharmacy, alum, glass salt, borax, camphor, corrosive sublimate, laboratory techniques</keywords>

306 *Chemistry*

you shall remove it and filter it. Some women use this ox gall <note>Bile taken from an ox, historically used in medicines and as a cleansing agent in cosmetics</note> prepared as I have just described; others have it distilled in a <term>Bain-Marie</term> <note>original: "au Bain"; a water bath used for gentle heating</note>. As for me, I find it better undistilled than distilled. It corrodes the skin <note>The author likely means it acts as a strong chemical exfoliant</note>; it is good for coarse complexions; it removes tanning and protects against tanning. One applies it in the evening when going to bed; it must be washed off in the morning with some good water, such as strawberry water or <term>Queen of Hungary water</term> <note>A famous alcohol-based perfume and restorative tonic, primarily made of distilled rosemary</note>. As for the froth remaining on the filter, it is good for nothing. I have previously advised against putting <term>Sublimate</term> <note>Corrosive sublimate or mercuric chloride; a highly toxic mercury compound often used in early modern cosmetics for its whitening effects</note> into these compositions, yet it is included in this one; I felt obliged to write it down to provide a faithful report of the composition and preparation of the ox gall of Montpellier <note>Montpellier was a renowned French center for medical and pharmaceutical knowledge</note>.

### Yellow head-dresses for daytime wear.

TAKE an egg yolk, and four spoonfuls of **spirit of wine** <note>original: *esprit de vin*; a high-proof distilled alcohol similar to brandy used as a solvent</note>; beat them well together with a spoon, and dip your **cornette** <term>a fashionable linen or lace head-dress with side flaps, commonly worn by women in the 17th century</term> into it, which you must have first cleaned three or four times with **rose-water**; and then let them dry in the shade.

### Another way to yellow the head-dresses.

TAKE the inner bark of **Barberry** wood <note>original: *Berberis* or *Espine-vinette*</note>; set it to soak in river water for twenty-four hours: when you see the water is yellow, dip your **cornettes** inside, and set them to dry without pressing them.

### Another.

YOU shall take ten grains of **Saffron**, and pour an ounce of **spirit of wine** over it; and when the spirit of wine is well colored, you shall pour it off by tilting the vessel <note>original: *par inclination*; a technique of decanting the liquid while leaving the sediment behind</note>, and add more, and continue until your **Saffron** yields no more dye: mix...

### CHAPTER XI.
### Of pastes, waters, & pomades for the hands.

#### Paste for the hands.

**TAKE** of <term>the four cold seeds</term> <note>original: "quatre semences froides." In early modern pharmacy, these were typically the seeds of cucumber, melon, gourd, and watermelon, prized for their cooling and moisturizing effects.</note> husked, four ounces of each; Pine nuts, two ounces; peeled sweet Almonds, half a pound. First, pound the four cold seeds: when they have become a paste, add the Pine nuts and incorporate them; then, add the Almonds little by little. It requires at least four hours of pounding to properly blend the whole mixture. Then, add two fresh egg yolks; incorporate everything well together, and the paste will be finished. Place it in a jar with a little sugar on top <note>Sugar was often used as a preservative in these preparations.</note>; it will keep for a long time. You must rub your hands with it every morning, then wipe them with a white cloth.

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for a moisturizing hand paste made from almond, pine nut, and "cold seeds," emphasizing the labor-intensive process of manual pounding to achieve the correct consistency.</summary>

### CHAPTER XII.

### On Ptisans.
<note>A ptisan (from the Greek *ptisane*) is a medicinal infusion or decoction, historically often based on barley water, used as a gentle restorative.</note>

#### Ptisane for gaining weight.
<note>In the 17th century, being very thin was often seen as a sign of wasting disease; "fattening" treatments were intended to restore vitality and "moisture" to the body.</note>

**TAKE** ivory shavings and **hartshorn** <note>deer antler</note>, one ounce of each; half an ounce of yellow amber; four ounces of Damascus raisins, from which you shall remove the seeds; a handful of wheat grains: boil everything in four pints of water, until it is reduced by one pint; then filter it. One must drink three or four glasses of this ptisan every day, and continue for forty days. Before using it, it is necessary to be purged <note>This refers to the standard medical practice of the time: using a laxative or emetic to "clear the humors" before beginning a new treatment.</note>.

#### Another ptisane that gains weight and induces sleep.

**TAKE** oat groats and barley flour, one pound of each; six Reinette apples <note>A famous variety of firm, fragrant French dessert apples.</note> cut into slices; put everything into a vessel

It should be drunk in the morning, three hours after dinner <note>In this period, 'dinner' refers to the midday meal.</note>, and in the evening when going to bed, a large glass each time. This <term>tisane<note>A medicinal herbal infusion, often made with barley or other grains, intended to be easy on the stomach.</note></term>, besides inducing sleep and encouraging weight gain <note>In historical medicine, "fattening" the body was often a goal for patients who were "wasting away" or "dry" due to illness.</note>, it moistens and refreshes. It is good for old people and for the young.

### CHAPTER XIII.
### Method for cleaning and dressing gloves.

TAKE thin and soft <note>The original French "pateux" literally means doughy, referring to leather that is supple and absorbent.</note> leather gloves; wash them in spring water fifteen or twenty times in succession, until the water comes out very clear; for the last time you shall wash them in Rose-water, and let them dry gently in the shade; then take fresh egg yolks, and soak the gloves in them, and rub them in such a way that the egg yolks penetrate the gloves through and through; afterwards soak them in orange blossom oil, or Jasmine oil—or in the absence of these, sweet Almond oil, or <term>oil of the four cold seeds</term> <note>In early modern pharmacy, the "four cold seeds" typically referred to a mixture of cucumber, melon, gourd, and pumpkin seeds, used for their cooling and soothing properties.</note>; spread the gloves on a piece of paper, and let them dry gently between two sheets of paper, and put them out in the air once or twice, for fear that they might spoil, and store them in a dry place.

<summary>This page provides a detailed recipe for cleaning and softening high-quality leather gloves using repeated washings, rose water, egg yolks, and floral oils.</summary>
<keywords>leather care, gloves, rose water, egg yolks, floral oils, oil of the four cold seeds, 18th-century chemistry, practical arts</keywords>

## Another way for gloves.

**P**URIFY your gloves as has been stated above, and rub them with fresh egg yolks: afterwards take two ounces of <term>kid-skin ointment</term> <note>original: "pommade de Chevreau." This was likely a fat-based salve made from or intended for delicate kid-leather.</note>, one ounce of yellow or white wax, and one ounce of sweet almond oil: melt everything together, and dip the gloves in it, and rub them, and incorporate the <note>The original term "drogues" (drugs) refers generally to the ingredients or raw materials used in the recipe, rather than medicine specifically.</note> drugs so well that they penetrate the gloves: afterward spread them out on white paper, and stretch them into the shape you want them to remain: pass a wooden roller over them to smooth them, and store them in a dry place. If you wish to perfume them, you will add some fragrant oil to the mixture, such as orange blossom or jasmine.

*Spirits or Essences suitable for the teeth.*

THE SPIRIT of Sulfur, of <term>Vitriol</term> <note>Spirit of Vitriol is a historical name for sulfuric acid.</note>, of <term>Sea Salt</term> <note>Spirit of Sea Salt is a historical name for hydrochloric acid.</note>, of <term>Saltpeter</term> <note>Spirit of Saltpeter is a historical name for nitric acid.</note>, and of Alum, whiten the teeth and corrode them, and remove <term>chancres</term> <note>In this context, chancres likely refers to mouth ulcers or canker sores.</note>, and make them clear and white. One must rub them lightly with a small stick, or roots, as will be described hereafter, because they would otherwise corrode and burn the flesh <note>The author correctly warns that these strong acids, while effective at stripping stains from enamel, are highly caustic and will cause chemical burns to the gums if not handled with care.</note>; one must wash the mouth afterward with warm wine.

*To prepare the roots and wood for rubbing the teeth.*

TAKE small sticks of Ivy, and roots of <term>Marshmallow</term> <note>Marshmallow root (Althaea officinalis) was commonly used in early medicine for its soothing, mucilaginous properties.</note>, and boil them in vinegar with a little Salt and Alum: and when the roots begin to soften, and the wood peels, remove them from the fire, and dry them gently; then use them to rub the teeth after meals.

->## CHAPTER XV.<-

->### Concerning hair dyes.<-

### Dye to make the hair blond.

**TAKE** copper filings, **rock salt** <note>original: "Sel gemme," referring to naturally occurring halite</note>, a half-pound of each; one pound of **bryony roots** <note>original: "Coulevrée," a climbing plant often used in early modern medicine and cosmetics</note>: cut the roots, & crush them, & put them into a <term>retort</term> <note>a glass vessel with a long neck used for distilling liquids</note>, along with the salt & filings: let them infuse for one night, then **distill** with a <term>circular fire</term> <note>original: "feu de rouë," a method of heating where fuel is placed in a ring around the vessel to provide steady, high heat</note> until no more smoke comes out. To use this water, one must dissolve as much <term>gum tragacanth</term> <note>a natural thickening agent derived from dried sap</note> in **rosewater** as it is able to dissolve: take one part of this water, & one part of the distilled water, & heat them a little, & moisten the hair with brushes, or a small paintbrush, & let it dry before combing.

To use this water, it must be applied slightly warm with a brush.

*Dye to make the hair black.*

TAKE one pound of oak galls <term>growths found on oak trees, highly valued in the past for their tannins used in making black ink and dyes</term>, cut them into pieces, and boil them in olive oil until they are soft: dry them, and crush them very well, and make a powder of them: mix with an equal part of willow charcoal powder <note>original: "charbon de Sault"—likely referring to "saule" or willow, commonly used for fine charcoal</note> one handful, of prepared and crushed common salt one handful; a few dried and powdered lemon and orange peels: you must boil everything in twelve pounds of water, until the ingredients <note>original: "drogues"—referring generally to the dry ingredients of the recipe</note> remain in the consistency of an oint- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page, likely intended to say "ointment."</note>

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of a distilled medicinal or cosmetic recipe and the beginning of a detailed formula for a black hair dye using oak galls and charcoal.</summary>

...ointment; with this ointment, you shall rub the hair, then put it under a cap to let it dry. When the hair is dry, you must comb it. This dye is excellent and strengthens the brain <note>In the 17th century, it was commonly believed that topical scalp treatments could "fortify" the brain by closing the pores or balancing the "humors" of the head.</note>; the hair never turns red. It must be applied once a month.

### Paste to dye the hair black.

**TAKE** two ounces of quicklime <note>calcium oxide, a caustic substance often used in early hair treatments</note>, slake it in enough water to reduce it to a powder: incorporate with the said lime powder one ounce of silver litharge <term>silver litharge: a mineral form of lead monoxide (PbO) produced during silver refining, historically used as a pigment and in medicinal plasters</term> well-washed two or three times in rose water and dried: incorporate the whole, and make a paste of it. You must rub the hair with it in the evening and comb it in the morning.

->**Pomade to make hair grow.**<-

TAKE chicken fat, hempseed oil <term>hempseed oil (original: huile de chennevié)</term>, and honey, four ounces of each: melt everything in an earthenware pot, and incorporate them together until they reach the consistency of a pomade. The head must be rubbed with this pomade for eight consecutive days.

->Charitable & Easy. 333<-

...apply it to the spot where you wish to remove the hair, and leave it there all night. You must continue this until the hair has fallen out. Distilled water made from the leaves and roots of **Celandine** <note>original: "Celidoine"; specifically *Chelidonium majus*, a plant in the poppy family traditionally used in folk medicine for skin conditions.</note>, applied in the same way, produces the same result.

## Lime Water for the same effect.

**L**ime water made from distilled quicklime works more quickly than the previous remedies; a single application is enough, but it is also more aggressive. Take **quicklime** <term>Quicklime: A caustic chemical (calcium oxide) produced by heating limestone. It is highly reactive when it comes into contact with water or skin.</term> as it comes fresh from the kiln, reduce it to a powder, and place it in a **Retort** <term>Retort: A spherical glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used in a laboratory for distilling substances.</term>, which you should fill three-quarters full, then distill it over a **wheel fire** <term>Wheel fire: An alchemical and chemical term (feu de roue) for a fire built in a circle around the vessel to provide intense, even heat.</term>. This process yields very little water. It must be applied with a feather to the area where you wish to remove the hair, taking great care not to get it anywhere else. After applying it, you must rub the area with ointment or with **oil of the four cold seeds** <note>A traditional medicinal preparation made from the "greater" cold seeds: cucumber, gourd, melon, and watermelon. It was used to soothe inflammation and "heat" on the skin.</note>; a single application is sufficient.

<summary>This page provides recipes for depilation, starting with a mild Celandine extract and moving to a potent, caustic distilled quicklime solution. The text warns of the lime's intensity and provides a cooling oil recipe to soothe the skin afterward.</summary>

<keywords>depilation, hair removal, quicklime, Celandine, distillation, retort, wheel fire, four cold seeds, dermatology, caustic remedies</keywords>

334 *Charitable & Easy Chemistry.*

*Ointment to remove the "flour" <note>This likely refers to dandruff or scurf, which appears as white flakes at the scalp.</note> that comes to the root of the hair.*

TAKE half a pound of pork fat, melt it in a small earthenware pot: incorporate it with <term>flowers of Sulfur</term> <note>A fine powder produced when sulfur is sublimated; used historically for skin conditions.</note> and <term>calcined Alum</term> <note>Alum that has been heated to remove its water content, making it more caustic or absorbent.</note>, one ounce of each: let it come to a boil, then strain and squeeze it out. You must rub the head with this ointment two or three times, and leave two or three days in between applications.

*Water to make the hair curl.*

TAKE one ounce of <term>gum Elemi</term> <note>A fragrant resin from certain trees, used here as a stiffening agent to hold curls.</note>, and put it to soak in one pound of rose-water, which you will boil for half a quarter of an hour <note>Approximately seven or eight minutes.</note>: when it is cold, you must moisten the hair with it, then put it in <term>curlers</term> <note>original: "papillottes"; these were typically small pieces of paper or fabric used to wrap hair for curling.</note>, or under a cap.

*THE END.*
